#5171. Analysts see no broader ‘contagion’ from $6.3B Binance hack

Binance’s pledge to cover all user losses helped contain the fallout from the $6.3 billion hack, analysts said.

Binance’s pledge to cover user losses from its $6.3 billion hack helped contain the fallout across crypto markets, analysts told Cointelegraph.

“There doesn’t seem to be any contagion-type effect from the hack, given that Binance is able to cover the losses using its balance sheet,” Martin Lee, market insights lead at DWF Labs, told Cointelegraph. He said investors turned their focus to the implications of the US midterm elections.

Binance’s daily net outflows fell to $458 million in the 24 hours leading to Monday’s publication time, down from $6.1 billion on Nov. 2 after withdrawals resumed, according to DefiLlama.

Attackers stole about $6.3 billion from Binance hot wallets in the Oct. 29 breach, making it the largest crypto hack to date. Bitcoin (BTC) fell as much as 5.7% over the first five days after the hack, a decline CryptoQuant called “modest” in its Wednesday report .

Bitcoin fell roughly 13.6% after the $1.4 billion Bybit hack in 2025 and about 9.9% after the Coincheck hack in 2018. At least 14 of the past 38 hacks CryptoQuant reviewed were followed by steeper Bitcoin declines.

Bitcoin’s drawdown after the Binance hack, compared to previous exchange hacks. Source: CryptoQuant

Major exchange hacks have previously triggered “short-lived drawdowns that markets later absorbed” as long-term holder demand returned, Lacie Zhang, research lead at Bitget Wallet, told Cointelegraph, adding that the Binance hack does not represent a “structural trend reversal” for crypto markets.

Related: Binance hackers had 8-week intrusion window before $6.3B exploit: SEAL

“Much of the hack’s impact now looks priced in,” with Bitcoin currently trading at an “attractive level,” Bitfinex analysts told Cointelegraph.

“Confirmation of a market bottom now depends on the Consumer Price Index (CPI) reading due on 10 November, which will shape the Federal Reserve’s next rate decision. Higher bond yields would put pressure on risk assets.”

Market participants priced in an 80% chance that the Federal Reserve will raise rates at its Dec. 9 meeting, up from 69% a month earlier, according to the CMEGroup’s FedWatch tool.

Bitcoin fell 2.5% over 24 hours to $74,580 as of 1:21 pm UTC on Monday, according to CoinMarketCap.

#5170. Treasury Cuts Crypto Exchange MEXC Off From US Financial System

Treasury Cuts Crypto Exchange MEXC Off From US Financial System

The U.S. Treasury Department has moved to sever crypto exchange MEXC Global from the American financial system, accusing it of facilitating at least $16 billion in illicit transactions tied to Iran, North Korea and Russia.

In an order issued Friday through its Financial Crimes Enforcement Network, Treasury said MEXC, one of the world’s 20 largest crypto exchanges, served as a major conduit for sanctions evasion and cybercrime.

The action, part of a broader effort dubbed Operation Economic Outcast, bars U.S. financial institutions from processing any funds transfers to or from the exchange. It takes effect 30 days after publication in the Federal Register.

“Financial institutions cannot have it both ways: Either you support the Iranian regime, or you have access to the U.S. financial system,” Treasury Secretary Scott Bessent said in a statement, vowing to keep severing the regime’s “economic lifelines” and its attempts to use digital assets to dodge sanctions.

Founded in 2018, MEXC processes more than $1 billion in daily transactions, holds around $5 billion in reserves, and offers more than 3,000 tokens, according to FinCEN. Between May 2021 and May 2026, FinCEN said, the platform moved at least $16 billion connected to illicit activity, including nearly $1.5 billion tied to Russian sanctions evasion, with $232 million in direct transactions with sanctioned entities. The agency named MEXC a “primary money laundering concern” in connection with Russian illicit finance.

Despite publicly claiming anti-money-laundering controls and barring users in places like Iran and North Korea, MEXC’s compliance was riddled with “severe deficiencies,” FinCEN said.

The exchange was still transacting with sanctioned Russian exchange Garantex and its successor Grinex as recently as June 2026, more than four years after Garantex was first designated. It also processed roughly $200 million linked to the A7A5 stablecoin and about $22 million benefiting Iran’s central bank and its Islamic Revolutionary Guard Corps.

Those connections place MEXC squarely within a sprawling sanctions-evasion ecosystem that Treasury has been dismantling piece by piece.

The department last month designated Russia’s A7 Network a transnational criminal organization and proposed cutting its front companies off from U.S. finance, flagging more than $179 billion in transactions through its ruble-backed A7A5 token, much of it routed through Garantex and Grinex.

The same campaign has reached into Iran’s crypto sector, where Treasury gained sweeping authority to sanction anyone operating in it and blacklisted the Iranian exchange Nobitex over its role in funneling crypto to the IRGC.

The order uses a provision of the Combating Russian Money Laundering Act rather than a standard sanctions designation, a tool FinCEN has increasingly reached for to cut off crypto infrastructure that operates outside the correspondent banking system.

#5162. Sony Axing Game Discs Will Have Outsized Impact on Low-Income Gamers, Experts Say

Sony Axing Game Discs Will Have Outsized Impact on Low-Income Gamers, Experts Say

Gamers in low-income areas will be among the hardest hit by Sony’s decision to stop producing physical game discs from 2028, experts told Decrypt, while one African games industry expert arguing the continent stands to lose out badly.

The secondary market has long been a cheap way for gamers to get hold of games. The PlayStation Store does run sales, but buying digitally assumes a decent internet connection, which rural households are less likely to have.

"By abandoning physical discs, some players at the bottom of the economic spectrum lose access to physical distribution through retail and, more importantly, discounted used sales," Joost van Dreunen, professor at the NYU Stern School of Business and video game business author, told Decrypt. "It won't immediately show up in the financials of major game makers, since their focus is now on creating premium experiences for a luxury class of gamers."

It comes as the cost of being a gamer is trending upwards. Sony raised PS5 prices in March, for example, citing "pressures in the global economic landscape." Similarly, the next generation of Xbox is expected to cost up to $1,800, according to Fortune.

A spokesperson for UK advocacy group Gamer's Voice told Decrypt that Sony's move to cut discs will "likely affect people on lower incomes more."

Isa Muhammad, founder of Africa and Middle East-focused games industry platform Bijetic, told Decrypt that the PS5 is already "out of reach for most Africans." He stressed that those who can buy a console still rely on the second-hand games market to afford to play on the consoles.

Well, at least the highest-income Africans could still take advantage of the PlayStation Store discounts, right? Unfortunately not. Muhammad said that the PlayStation Network is only supported in one African nation: South Africa. Many other nations are missing from the PlayStation Network's official list of supported countries.

"A gamer in Nigeria who wants to buy digitally has to set up an account registered in the U.S. or UK, then pay with foreign gift cards or a dollar card," Muhammad said.

Download speeds in rural, remote, and low-income areas are another major hurdle.

"Bandwidth is now part of what a game costs," van Dreunen said. "Roughly one in four rural [American] households has no wired provider offering 100/20 Mbps, and at 10 Mbps a 50GB download takes about 11 hours."

In Africa and the Middle East, Muhammad said, internet speeds vary significantly across the region—from super-fast connections in Dubai to unreliable ones in Iran. Internet access is also costly in some countries.

"Just recently I paid 75,000 NGN (around $55) for a 450GB broadband bundle, which is more than Nigeria's minimum monthly wage," Muhammad told Decrypt. With GTA 6's pre-load file expected to be 212GB, "a gamer here would first spend $80 on the game, then anywhere from $25 to over $60 just to download it, depending on how they buy data."

And at Nigeria's median download speed of 36.77 Mbps, per Speedtest by Ookla, the download would take nearly 13 hours. Not to mention the fact that they'd need to have a non-Nigerian PlayStation account, a download code corresponding to the country their account is supposedly from, and a way to pay in the local currency.

"Sony's own terms say you must register your account in the country where you live, and Sony can suspend or close accounts created with false information," Muhammad told Decrypt. "So every Nigerian or Egyptian on a US or UK PSN account is breaking the rules from day one."

African gamers live in fear of needing help from Sony and being refused it (or worse, banned) because their accounts break its rules, Muhammad explained. In a future where every purchase is digital, it could open the door to gamers losing accounts that hold every game they've ever purchased.

"If a player in Africa buys GTA 6 and can't recover their account, they may have no choice but to buy it again on a new one, along with everything else they've lost," he said. "A disc-free future will see this become the reality for every new PlayStation game, for gamers across most of Africa."

Sony and PlayStation did not respond to Decrypt's request for comment.

#5157. AI Chip Stocks Bounce After Global Rout—But Is the Bubble Starting to Burst?

AI Chip Stocks Bounce After Global Rout—But Is the Bubble Starting to Burst?

AI stocks staged a comeback Friday after a rout that swept from Wall Street to Asia. But a single green session hardly settles the bigger question for investors heading into the weekend: Is this an overdue correction, or the beginning of an AI bubble bursting?

Nvidia rose 2.78% to $186.64, after plunging 9.3% this Thursday. AMD also bounced to $449.04 following a 17.3% collapse a day earlier. Both stocks remain well below their October peaks, showing how little a sharp relief rally can repair.

It wasn’t just U.S. markets seeing red either. South Korea’s KOSPI lost 6% Friday, while Taiwan’s benchmark dropped more than 8% during the week—its worst week in months. TSMC hit its daily limit-down threshold during the rout. Reuters reported that Taiwanese officials asked state-owned banks to help support prices.

The US Tech 100 cash-market proxy also recovered, gaining 1.85% to 27,007.3, which means it’s down more than 5.5% this week. Its daily candles show the bounce arriving after a much larger slide from October levels, not a return to the previous trend.

The U.S. midterms accelerated fears that Democratic gains would mean tougher scrutiny of data centers. In 40 of 41 local ballot contests on November 3, voters favored limiting development, including by rejecting pro-construction measures, Ballotpedia found. Some votes were advisory, but the political message was unmistakable.

That matters for chip companies because data centers are their biggest growth story. Each project requires vast spending on processors, servers, power and cooling. Restrictions could delay orders or force construction elsewhere.

Yet the doubts predated the election. AI companies and their customers are spending aggressively to build capacity, often before returns are clear. More expensive borrowing makes that bet harder to sustain. Reuters quoted market strategist Steve Sosnick warning that heavily owned stocks were already “priced to perfection.”

A correction means a market has fallen at least 10% from a recent peak. It does not necessarily mean the underlying business is shrinking. Imagine a chipmaker whose shares trade at 60 times annual earnings because investors expect explosive growth. If investors become willing to pay only 40 times those same earnings, the stock can lose a third of its value without profits falling at all.

A bubble bursting is more serious: It happens when expectations prove unrealistic and the money supporting the boom begins to disappear.

History offers a useful warning. During the dot-com crash, the Nasdaq lost roughly 77% between March 2000 and October 2002. The internet was not a fad, but investors had vastly overestimated how quickly companies could turn it into profit.

Telecom operators spent fortunes laying fiber-optic cables for customers who had yet to arrive. Global Crossing, which built an international network, filed for bankruptcy in 2002. The technology survived; many companies and shareholders did not.

AI could face a similar mismatch between long-term promise and near-term returns—and some investors are already talking about it. For example, the RAM shortage is also a business opportunity that many companies are trying to use for their advantage. Former crypto miners are renting their hardware and cloud provider and inference startups are appearing everywhere.

Now, consider a hypothetical company borrowing billions to build a data center packed with Nvidia chips, expecting to rent that computing power at premium prices for years. If customers spend less than anticipated, borrowing becomes more expensive or construction is delayed, the debt still has to be repaid.

The operator may cancel chip orders, its contractors lose work, and utilities that planned new capacity lose anticipated customers. Lenders and investors holding its debt could also take losses. A stock-market selloff can then become a problem for the wider economy.

There is an important difference from 2000, however. Microsoft, Alphabet, Amazon and Meta are profitable businesses with established customers and substantial cash flows, unlike many of the speculative dot-com startups. They can withstand setbacks better than smaller AI-cloud providers that depend on repeatedly borrowing to expand.

It is possible for AI to keep transforming industries while a bunch of individual AI companies fail—and for investors in successful companies to have overpaid.

The financial warning lights are beginning to flicker. Reuters reported Friday that the cost of insuring against a default by AI-cloud provider CoreWeave had climbed above 1,000 basis points. Such protection works somewhat like insurance on a loan: as the price rises, investors are signaling greater concern about repayment.

Yet broader bond markets were still operating relatively normally. That points to growing strain in the riskiest corners of AI financing, not proof of a system-wide crisis.

Next week will offer more concrete tests than another day of red or green candles. Results from CoreWeave, Applied Materials, and Cisco could reveal whether customers are still ordering capacity and equipment, while inflation data will influence expectations for borrowing costs. Stable orders and manageable debt would support the correction argument.

Repeated canceled projects, failed refinancing and shrinking sales would suggest the boom's financial foundations are cracking. The question is not whether AI has a future, but whether every company betting billions on that future can afford to get there.

#5151. Apple Completes the Set: Every Magnificent Seven Stock Opts Out of OKX-ICE Venue

Apple Completes the Set: Every Magnificent Seven Stock Opts Out of OKX-ICE Venue

Every member of Wall Street’s “Magnificent Seven” now wants its stock kept off OKX and ICE’s planned tokenized stock venue.

Apple and Meta Platforms are among 11 companies added to the list of objecting issuers in an updated notice from OKXICE TSV dated Thursday.

They join Microsoft, Alphabet, Amazon, Nvidia and Tesla, completing a sweep by the group of tech giants that makes up a large share of the S&P 500’s value. The other new objectors are Home Depot, Boeing, GE Vernova, Palantir, ServiceNow, S&P Global, KLA, Super Micro Computer and Nebius Group, bringing the total number of companies saying “no” to the OKX and ICE platform to 67.

Meta’s objection carries some irony. The Facebook parent once tried to launch its own stablecoin, Libra, later renamed Diem, before abandoning the project in 2022 amid regulatory pushback.

Apple had been one of the last megacaps missing from the list after Amazon, Tesla, and SpaceX objected in the venue’s Nov. 2 notice. The tally stood at 56 then, and at nine on Oct. 15, when the first objectors appeared.

Just as notable is who hasn’t objected. Crypto-friendly public companies such as Coinbase, Robinhood and Strategy don’t appear on the list. Coinbase launched tokenized stocks for non-U.S. users on its Base network in August. Berkshire Hathaway, Visa, and Exxon Mobil are also absent. It’s unclear whether OKXICE notified those companies.

Under the SEC’s innovation exemption, which the agency unveiled in September after the Clarity Act failed to advance in the Senate, a venue must notify a company before offering a version of its shares tokenized by an unaffiliated third party. If the company objects in writing within 30 days, the venue can’t make that stock available for trading, according to the SEC’s order. It’s worth noting, though, that the objection applies only to that venue.

OKXICE TSV, a 50-50 joint venture between NYSE owner Intercontinental Exchange and OKX’s U.S. holding company, plans to offer tokenized U.S. stocks 24/7 through permissioned pools on OKX’s XLayer blockchain. An outside “tokenizer” would hold real shares one-for-one through a registered broker-dealer.

With the market’s biggest names opted out, it’s unclear which major stocks the venue will be able to offer at launch, and its notices don’t list the tokens it plans to make available.

#5148. Bitcoin, tech stocks brush off Nvidia nerves as analyst mulls $68K target

Bitcoin rebounded from an 11-week low near $73,500 as the Nasdaq Composite rallied despite sell-off in Nvidia shares.

Bitcoin (BTC) returned above $75,000 after Monday’s Wall Street open as tech stocks rebounded.

Data from TradingView showed BTC/USD up 0.8%, having dipped as low as $73,500 on Sunday, a level not seen since Aug. 21.

BTC/USD one-hour chart. Source: Cointelegraph/TradingView

The weakness accompanied news that Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang may have been the target of a Chinese intelligence effort, a claim Beijing subsequently denied. Nvidia stock fell about 6% to $170.24 on Monday early trading, a level it last traded at on March 31, before rebounding to session highs of $179.92.

Nvidia stock one-day chart. Source: Cointelegraph/TradingView

In a Sunday note, Dan Ives, senior managing director at merchant bank Yorkville Ives & Co., warned that the episode could create lasting headwinds for Nvidia’s share price.

“This is a black eye moment for Jensen and will spark multiple congressional inquiries at a minimum and will create an overhang on the stock for a myriad of reasons,” he said, according to CNBC.

Gary Gensler, former chair of the US Securities and Exchange Commission (SEC), said that Nvidia’s board would face “tough choices” based on the results of the company’s internal investigation.

Elsewhere, investors sought to buy the lows on tech stocks, with the tech-heavy Nasdaq Composite Index shaking off the Nvidia claims to gain 2.5% in the first hour of the US trading session.

Nasdaq Composite Index one-hour chart. Source: Cointelegraph/TradingView

Falling oil prices helped spur renewed strength in equities and offered Bitcoin some respite from its own downside. US WTI crude fell to $86.19 per barrel on the day, its lowest since Sep. 1, before recovering to around $88.20.

CFDs on WTI crude oil one-day chart. Source: Cointelegraph/TradingView

In his latest analysis, economist Robin Brooks reiterated gold, Bitcoin and the S&P 500’s negative correlation to oil following the attack on Saudi Arabia’s East-West Pipeline, a key oil-transit route, in September.

“Even if these correlations fade somewhat going forward, it’s reasonable to think that a fall in oil prices will lift these risk assets,” he summarized.

BTC/USD acted in a narrow range between its 21-week and 50-week simple moving averages (SMA) on Monday, with these at $73,078 and $75,492, respectively.

Commenting on current market structure, Keith Alan, cofounder of crypto trading platform Material Indicators, eyed a potential dip toward the 200-week SMA at $67,755, a trend line that last interacted with price in mid-August. At the same time, Alan saw conditions slowly starting to improve on weekly time frames.

“The macro structure and thin order books leave room for lower prices before a breakout can be validated. That test is possible, not inevitable - and it doesn’t have to happen this week. In fact, the weekly view is starting to lean bullish,” he told X followers.

BTC/USD one-day chart with 21, 50, 200 SMA. Source: Cointelegraph/TradingView

#5146. This Bitcoin Developer’s Idea Would Make Quantum Attacks a Losing Bet

This Bitcoin Developer’s Idea Would Make Quantum Attacks a Losing Bet

Bitcoin developer Cory Fields has an unusual idea for dealing with future quantum thieves: Make them risk their own Bitcoin every time they try to steal someone else’s.

In a November 4 post to the Bitcoin development mailing list, Fields outlined “quantum landmines” and two types of auctions designed to turn attacks on vulnerable wallets into expensive gambles. He called the ideas “thought experiments,” not a completed Bitcoin upgrade.

Bitcoin owners use a secret private key to authorize transactions. A matching public key lets the network check their signatures without learning the secret.

If the whole idea seems too hard to understand, we used AI to generate a video to help you grasp it:

Today’s computers cannot realistically calculate that private key from the public one. A sufficiently powerful quantum computer could use a mathematical technique called Shor’s algorithm to do it, potentially letting a thief spend coins as if they owned them.

Some older Bitcoin outputs have exposed public keys, and certain newer formats reveal them too. Other addresses hide the key until someone spends, so the risk is not identical for every wallet.

Researchers call the hypothetical arrival of a computer capable of these attacks “Q-Day.” No publicly demonstrated machine can steal Bitcoin this way today, and nobody knows exactly when one might exist.

Imagine someone owns 1 BTC in an old, potentially vulnerable wallet. Before Q-Day, this person creates a tiny, separate quantum-safe Bitcoin output containing a hidden proof that they controlled the original private key while quantum theft was still impossible.

Think of it as a sealed ownership receipt. Outsiders can see the small output, but not the evidence tucked inside it, or even that it is a trap.

Now imagine a future thief uses a quantum computer to recover the user’s old private key. Under Fields’s suggested rules, the thief cannot simply take her 1 BTC: They must add 1 BTC of their own from a quantum-safe wallet as collateral.

Both coins enter a restricted output worth 2 BTC. The thief must wait through a challenge period—30 days in Fields’s example—before spending it freely.

The wallet owner notices the transaction and reveals the pre-Q-Day proof. The network then allows the owner to move the full 2 BTC to the quantum-safe address they committed to earlier: their original coin, plus the thief’s collateral.

The attacker has no way to know in advance which old wallets contain these hidden receipts. Even a successful quantum key recovery could become a 1 BTC loss.

The wallet owner would also need matching collateral to initiate a normal post-Q-Day move of her own legacy coin. If they have no spare BTC, the same rule intended to hurt thieves could make moving her savings difficult.

Landmines only work when an owner prepares one before Q-Day and reveals it in time. They cannot protect coins whose keys are already lost, including some of the early Bitcoin holdings widely believed to be inaccessible.

Fields proposes a second mechanism called a staked auction. Before Q-Day, someone could lock up quantum-safe Bitcoin and secretly register a bid against a specific old wallet they expect thieves to target.

Suppose an apparently abandoned wallet holds 50 BTC. A future attacker tries to move it and posts another 50 BTC as collateral; during the auction period, someone who committed a bid in advance can challenge the attempted theft.

In Fields’s example, a successful challenger claims part of the attacker’s collateral while the original vulnerable coins are destroyed rather than awarded to the bidder. The point is to deprive thieves of a reliable payday, not return the coins to a missing owner.

His third idea, a sats auction, requires no advance stake. Instead, a vulnerable-coin spend would trigger a contest in which the highest bidder gets the coins minus their bid, while the runner-up pays their bid and gets nothing.

That resembles a notorious auction game where two bidders keep raising their offers because neither wants to be the person who loses money for nothing. A thief might avoid starting a contest that could cost more than the coins they hoped to steal.

One proposed response to quantum risk is to restrict spending from outdated wallets after a migration deadline. That might prevent theft, but could also permanently lock out legitimate owners who never moved their coins.

Fields is exploring a different trade-off: Keep old coins potentially spendable, but make attempted transfers costly and open to challenges. Bitcoin cannot tell an honest owner from a quantum thief if both can produce the same valid signature.

The trade-off is severe, considering that honest owners might need large collateral deposits, inactive holders could miss their chance to object, and auction rules might burn coins belonging to people who are still alive and simply offline.

Bitcoin would first need a quantum-safe way to hold and spend funds. Wallets would then need to support hidden ownership commitments, while Bitcoin’s consensus rules would have to enforce collateral, waiting periods, special recovery transactions, and auctions.

Developers would also need to agree on when the new rules activate, how much collateral is required, and what happens to losing bids or leftover funds. Fields believes some mechanics could be introduced through a soft fork, a type of Bitcoin rule change, but has not supplied the detailed design.

None of these protections exists in Bitcoin today. Fields has published no implementation or activation schedule, and his original post leaves the auction payouts and even the desired outcome of an auction unresolved.

River Markets said its affiliate, River Finance LLC, has registered with the Commodity Futures Trading Commission as a futures commission merchant (FCM) and joined the National Futures Association (NFA), allowing it to carry customer accounts and handle client funds.

The company is building a prime brokerage platform for prediction markets that aims to let institutional traders manage capital, positions, and risk across multiple exchanges and clearinghouses through a single account, with plans to support financing arrangements and permitted over-the-counter trading.

"Our conversations with the institutions entering this market give us a unique view of what they need," Cofounder and CEO Oscar Levy said in a release. "Access to event contracts isn’t enough. River Markets was founded in May and serves hedge funds, sports funds, proprietary trading firms, and event-risk hedging companies, according to the firm. River Markets has routed over 250 million contracts and is backed by "top-tier venture capital firms" following its $8.5 million seed funding round.

Combined trading volume across Kalshi, Polymarket US, and Polymarket's international platform exceeded $500 billion in October. Prediction markets are becoming large enough to create demand for the kinds of brokerage, clearing, financing, and risk-management services traditionally associated with derivatives markets.

In July, Polymarket-affiliated entities sought an FCM and swap-firm registration through the NFA to enable U.S. customers to trade using borrowed capital. Kalshi affiliate Kinetic Markets received NFA approval as an FCM and swap firm in March. And last month, Blockchain.com applied for both a designated contract market (DCM) license and FCM registration.

Of note, Hyperliquid Strategies last week filed to become an FCM to carry U.S. client accounts for HIP-3 perpetual futures markets, an effort to help bring permissioned Hyperliquid markets to U.S. customers within a regulated framework.

#5143. Avalanche’s AVAX leads crypto gains following NYSE tokenization deal

Avalanche’s AVAX token rose as much as 10% after the NYSE selected its blockchain to support settlement on a planned tokenized stock trading platform.

Avalanche’s AVAX token jumped as much as 10% on Monday, making it the top earner among the 100 largest cryptocurrencies by market capitalization, as the New York Stock Exchange (NYSE) announced plans to use its blockchain to settle trades on a new platform for trading tokenized stocks around the clock.

AVAX later eased to $8.86, still up over 8% on the day, according to CoinGecko data as of 03:22 p.m. UTC. The token currently has a market cap of around $3.95 billion, with $439.7 million traded over the past day. The Avalanche blockchain boasts a total value locked (TVL) of $633 million, up 4% over the past 24 hours, according to DeFiLlama.

“At the NYSE, we’re looking at how blockchain technology can expand what we offer in equities, and we’re picking the right technology for each job,” Dennis Lee, the head of digital issuance at NYSE, said in an announcement.

The deal underscores a growing effort by Wall Street firms to use blockchain technology to make stock trading faster and allow investors to trade outside regular market hours. Typically, traditional US stock markets are open from 9:30 am to 4:00 pm EST on weekdays, with trades often taking at least one business day to settle.

The NYSE first announced plans for the platform in January. In September, Ava Labs revealed that the exchange had spent about a year testing Avalanche, but the blockchain’s final pick was not confirmed until Monday.

Related: Aave launches V4 on Avalanche, laying groundwork for tokenized credit markets

Ava Labs said that the NYSE will use Avalanche’s blockchain to keep track of stock trades and help complete transactions on its planned trading platform. The platform will let investors buy and sell digital versions of stocks on different blockchains, while Avalanche will be used to record those trades and make sure transactions are completed.

“The upcoming digital trading platform will support equities listed on a range of blockchains, though the ledger that records and settles trades needs to be fast and reliable, let us control who can take part, keep costs predictable, and have a large community of developers behind it. Avalanche meets all those needs,” Lee said.

The platform could launch before the end of 2026 if it receives regulatory approval, according to The Wall Street Journal.

“Our first conversations with the NYSE team began nearly a year ago, and it’s remarkable how quickly we’ve progressed toward supporting this infrastructure,” John Nahas, chief business officer at Ava Labs told Cointelegraph. “It’s an important step toward blockchain becoming foundational financial infrastructure.”

The news comes as other major financial firms are also investing in blockchain technology to make stock trading faster and available around the clock.

In September, Nasdaq announced plans to invest $100 million in Kraken’s parent company, Payward, to launch tokenized stocks. The expected launch for that project is the second quarter of 2027.

Meanwhile, the Depository Trust & Clearing Corporation (DTCC), which helps settle US securities trades, launched a service in October that allows financial firms to turn traditional securities into digital tokens.

“Today marks the culmination of years of work to bring tokenized securities into a regulated framework supported by trusted market infrastructure,” Frank La Salla, CEO of DTCC, said at the time. “By extending DTC’s resiliency, governance and operational standards into digital markets, we are establishing the foundation for responsible institutional adoption at scale.”

The NYSE deal also follows other financial firms’ efforts to bring traditional financial assets specifically onto Avalanche. In July, Japanese financial technology company Progmat announced it had moved more than 452 billion yen worth of digital securities onto a dedicated Avalanche blockchain.

And in September, Aave Labs announced plans to launch a lending service on Avalanche that would let financial firms borrow Tether’s USAT stablecoin using tokenized assets as collateral.

Nvidia's board of directors has opened an independent investigation following reports that CEO Jensen Huang was the target of a foreign intelligence effort, the company said on Sunday.

A committee of independent directors, assisted by outside counsel, will oversee the inquiry. "While the Board has no reason to believe that either national security or NVIDIA secrets were compromised, the Board's inquiry will examine all available information," it said, adding that it "has confidence in Jensen and his leadership team."

We just issued a press release from our board of directors. Read the release: https://t.co/W9yXWJzbkk

— NVIDIA Newsroom (@nvidianewsroom) November 8, 2026

The investigation follows a Financial Times report on Saturday that a U.S. government memo, written earlier this year based on intelligence information, said the Nvidia CEO had been romantically involved with Alice Huang, an executive at Singapore-based Nvidia customer Megaspeed, citing people familiar with the memo. U.S. intelligence believes she was recruited by China's Ministry of State Security, the FT reported. Nvidia declined to comment to the newspaper on the memo. Huang's email to staff, sent on Sunday, did not name the person with whom he had an affair.

Lawmakers from both parties are seeking answers. Republican leaders have asked Huang to appear privately before the House China, foreign affairs and intelligence committees, according to the FT, while Sen. Chris Coons (D-DE), who will chair the Senate Foreign Relations Committee from January, pledged in a tweet that "Congress will investigate and get answers."

The FT's report built on a New York Times investigation last month, which said U.S. intelligence agencies had concluded that Alice Huang, also known as Huang Le, a Chinese citizen, was a Chinese intelligence asset, and that surveillance of her had revealed a close relationship with Jensen Huang. President Donald Trump was briefed on the findings roughly a year ago and chose not to act on them, according to the Times.

U.S. officials suspected Megaspeed of helping Chinese companies get around restrictions on advanced AI chips, and the Commerce Department's investigation into the company has been handed to the Justice Department, the Times reported. Megaspeed's sister company in Malaysia bought at least $4.8 billion worth of AI equipment containing Nvidia chips between June 2024 and February 2026, according to trade records cited by the newspaper.

Nvidia told the Times that Huang's interactions with Ms. Huang were "not unusual or unexpected," and that customers like Megaspeed receive "ordinary business information." Megaspeed said it had retained outside counsel to conduct an internal investigation, Ms. Huang did not respond to the newspaper's requests for comment, and China's embassy in Washington dismissed the allegations as "groundless smears and slanders."

In an email to employees on Sunday, Huang apologized to his family and colleagues. "I was unfaithful to my wife. My actions hurt her and our children. There is no excuse for what I did," he wrote, according to a copy published by CNBC.

"Today's news will not slow NVIDIA. Jensen is committed to continuing to lead NVIDIA as CEO without interruption," the company told CNBC.

Nvidia shares were down 5% at $177.32 in premarket trading early on Monday, after closing at $186.64 on Friday, according to Yahoo Finance.

#5137. Trump Says He Knows 'Nothing' About World Liberty Financial as He Defends UAE Ties

Trump Says He Knows 'Nothing' About World Liberty Financial as He Defends UAE Ties

U.S. President Donald Trump has defended his family's stake in crypto venture World Liberty Financial, saying he knows "nothing" about the firm despite being a co-owner, in an interview after last week's midterm elections.

Asked by columnist Ross Douthat why his co-ownership of a company partly owned by investors linked to the United Arab Emirates government was different from the kind of arrangement he would call corrupt if it involved the Biden family, Trump replied: "Here's the difference [... ] I'm gonna tell you why. Because I know nothing about it," according to a transcript published by CBS News.

Pressed on why he did not simply divest, Trump said most of his money is managed by Wall Street firms through index funds, and that his sons run the family business. "It's hard to say to them, 'Don't do anything anymore for four years because I'm president,'" he said.

When Douthat asked whether foreign investors in the company believe they are buying influence, Trump said: "I don't know." He argued that conflicts of interest have dogged presidents "going back to George Washington," adding that past presidents "own something, whatever crypto was at the time."

"There's crypto, and before that there was oil," Trump said, adding that, "every president has had this problem."

"I'm a bigger name than other presidents, whether we like it or not," Trump said.

World Liberty Financial, which Eric Trump announced in August 2024, issues the WLFI governance token and the USD1 stablecoin. In January, it launched World Liberty Markets, a lending and borrowing platform built on DeFi protocol Dolomite.

Its UAE links have drawn scrutiny from Democrats. Aryam Investment, a UAE-backed entity tied to the country's national security adviser, Sheikh Tahnoon bin Zayed, agreed to buy a 49% stake in World Liberty Financial for $500 million four days before Trump's January 2025 inauguration, Decrypt reported in February, citing The Wall Street Journal. The deal, signed by Eric Trump on World Liberty's behalf, reportedly preceded the administration's reversal of restrictions on advanced AI chip sales to the UAE. A World Liberty spokesperson said at the time that neither Trump nor his Middle East envoy, Steve Witkoff, had "any involvement whatsoever" in the transaction, ABC News reported. Sen. Chris Murphy (D-CT) described the arrangement in February as "potentially criminal conduct." Trump has previously said he was not aware of the deal.

The company's UAE ties have also surfaced in its legal battle with Tron founder Justin Sun. Sun's lawyers asked a federal court to sanction World Liberty, alleging the firm delayed disclosing UAE ownership that could defeat federal jurisdiction over his lawsuit. World Liberty has called Sun's legal campaign a "meritless PR stunt."

The Trump family's financial stake in the venture is set out in World Liberty's own disclosures. DT Marks DEFI LLC, an entity affiliated with Trump and certain family members, owns about 38% of the holding company behind World Liberty Financial, holds 22.5 billion WLFI tokens, and is entitled to 75% of the net proceeds from WLFI token sales, according to the company. A separate Trump-affiliated entity has an indirect claim on interest earned on the reserves backing USD1. The disclosures state that no member of the Trump family is an officer, director or employee of World Liberty, though Trump's sons have remained involved with the company, Decrypt reported earlier this year.

PNC Bank, the eighth-largest US bank by assets, launched a limited pilot enabling eligible corporate and institutional clients to receive USDC payments through Coinbase.

USDC is the second-largest stablecoin by market capitalization, at $70.5 billion, behind Tether’s USDT at $176.8 billion, according to DefiLlama data. The total stablecoin market stood at around $290.8 billion on Monday.

#5134. Binance hack launderers mint homemade Elon Musk, animal memecoins to move stolen funds: Elliptic

Get The Block’s latest crypto news and price alerts for the coins you follow, right in Chrome. Add our free extension here.

Six throwaway tokens helped obfuscate stolen NEAR as trading proceeds, repeating a tactic used after the 2025 Bybit hack.

North Korea's money launderers are spinning up throwaway memecoins to help obfuscate funds stolen in the $6.4 billion Binance hack, according to blockchain analytics firm Elliptic.

In a new report released Monday, Elliptic said about 117,000 NEAR, worth roughly $430,000 at the time, were routed into six newly created memecoins created between Nov. 5 and Nov. 7.

Like many other memecoins, the tokens feature animal mascots and one sporting an expletive ticker. Another features Elon Musk under an "Elon 2028 President" banner. Elliptic added that there was no suggestion that Musk himself was linked to it.

The launderers can then use decentralized exchange liquidity pools to separate wallets holding stolen funds from wallets taking in proceeds. One set of wallets will buy the memecoins with stolen NEAR, while another sells the token into the pool and collects NEAR in return.

"At no point do the stolen funds move directly from a buying wallet to a collecting wallet, because the pool sits in between," Elliptic wrote. "To a casual observer, the collecting wallets look like ordinary traders who have sold a token at a profit."

The tokens have all since been abandoned, with Elliptic adding that much of the NEAR collected went into stablecoins or cross-chain bridges.

Elliptic noted that this same tactic was used following the 2025 Bybit hack, with launderers moving around $24 million through a homemade token paired with a stablecoin on Tron.

In the Binance case, the firm also found evidence of a similar approach on Solana using existing tokens that had low liquidity.

The transfers are still public and traceable on the blockchain. Elliptic said the additional swaps are only meant to be hurdles to slow investigators down, giving the launderers a little extra time to move funds before their wallets get flagged.

The amounts moved through the six NEAR tokens are still small compared to the 11.8 million NEAR worth around $42.7 million at current prices.

This follows Elliptic's update from Friday, which tracked roughly $124 million in stolen assets converted into bitcoin. Binance's earlier incident report said that the attack had been in the works for months and involved malicious developer software, a fake job interview, and compromised AI tooling.

The exchange said the FBI linked the attack to North Korea.

#5132. U.S. Regulators Drop $250 Travel Rule Plan, Crypto Language Too

FinCEN and the Federal Reserve have formally withdrawn a 2020 proposal that would have cut the Bank Secrecy Act threshold for cross-border funds transfers from $3,000 to $250 and spelled out that the rules cover crypto, leaving the existing $3,000 line in place.

The notice, published Monday in the Federal Register, says the Financial Crimes Enforcement Network and the Fed’s Board of Governors “will not take further action” on a proposal they first issued on Oct. 27, 2020.

That plan would have lowered the recordkeeping and Travel Rule thresholds to $250 for transfers that begin or end outside the United States. Those rules require banks and nonbank financial institutions to collect, retain and pass along customer information on transfers of $3,000 or more.

It also would have clarified the meaning of “money” in both rules so they apply to transactions involving convertible virtual currency, along with digital assets that have legal tender status to a recipient.

Per the notcie, FinCEN and the Fed received nearly 3,000 comments on the proposal, most of them substantially similar form letters. Commenters generally opposed the lower threshold, arguing that it would burden smaller institutions and could push consumers sending remittances toward less regulated channels.

Many also noted that potentially illicit transfers below $3,000 were already being flagged to FinCEN through suspicious activity reports. Several said the threshold should rise instead, citing inflation since 1995.

Sentiment on the crypto language was mixed. Companies that provide crypto services and their trade groups opposed it, citing technical limits and the lack of an international messaging standard for crypto transfers, while some banks and money services businesses supported it.

The agencies gave two reasons for pulling the proposal. More than five years have passed, and they said more public feedback would help before adopting anything similar. The withdrawal, they wrote, gives the public “certainty” that no final rule will be issued without another chance to comment.

The second reason is the crypto industry itself. The notice says crypto markets “have continued to mature” and new legal frameworks are emerging, citing the GENIUS Act stablecoin law enacted on July 18, 2025. Any future change tied to crypto, the agencies said, should be considered in light of those developments so it is “fit for purpose.”

The notice does not repeal or amend any rule. The $3,000 threshold that has applied for decades stays where it is, and so does the existing Travel Rule.

What disappears is the specific path to $250 and the proposed wording that would have written crypto into the definition of money. The agencies did not say whether they plan a new proposal or offer a timeline.

It does not signal a retreat from enforcement. On Friday, FinCEN cut off the MEXC exchange from the U.S. financial system over alleged sanctions evasion, using a different legal tool aimed at one named platform instead of a rule covering every institution.

Stablecoins are following their own track. Under the GENIUS Act, the Fed in June proposed customer identification requirements for stablecoin issuers, and FinCEN and the Treasury’s sanctions office proposed anti-money-laundering programs for the same firms in April.

For bitcoin users, the practical effect is that nothing changes: the $3,000 trigger stays, and the crypto-specific wording that industry groups opposed is gone for now.

Whether FinCEN and the Fed return with a new threshold, a crypto definition or neither will not be clear until one is published, and the agencies have committed to taking comments first.

#5131. Coinbase Joins Circle, Ripple With Final OCC Approval for Federal Trust Bank Charter

Coinbase Joins Circle, Ripple With Final OCC Approval for Federal Trust Bank Charter

Coinbase has received final approval from the Office of the Comptroller of the Currency (OCC) to charter Coinbase National Trust Company, the crypto exchange said on Monday.

The OCC approved the application last Friday, according to Coinbase. The firm said the charter will let it expand its custody business, which holds digital assets on behalf of institutional clients, under a federal regulatory framework.

"Custody is foundational to our business," the company said, describing itself as the leading crypto custodian globally. The charter will "bring clear and consistent federal uniformity" to its custody operations, it added.

"Trusted custody is essential for bringing more of the financial system onchain," Coinbase CEO Brian Armstrong tweeted on Monday, calling the trust company "another step for building that foundation."

Trusted custody is essential for bringing more of the financial system onchain.

Coinbase National Trust Company is another step for building that foundation. https://t.co/35K2yC3xpj

— Brian Armstrong (@brian_armstrong) November 9, 2026

Coinbase has offered regulated custody since 2018, when its Coinbase Custody Trust Company won a limited purpose trust charter from the New York Department of Financial Services. The new national trust company builds on that foundation while bringing its trust activities into a consistent federal framework, Coinbase said.

The approval comes seven months after the OCC gave Coinbase conditional approval in April, when the company said it was not becoming a commercial bank and would not take retail deposits or engage in fractional reserve banking. Coinbase first applied for the charter last year.

Coinbase joins a growing list of crypto firms with national trust charters. Circle won final approval in July for a trust bank to manage the reserves behind its USDC stablecoin, and Ripple received final approval last month for a trust bank behind its RLUSD stablecoin.

Others are still awaiting final approval. Jack Dorsey's Block has applied for a charter to custody Bitcoin in September, Kraken parent Payward filed its own application in May, and a trust company linked to Trump family-backed World Liberty Financial won conditional approval in August for a charter tied to its USD1 stablecoin, Reuters reported.

The approvals have drawn opposition. Sen. Elizabeth Warren (D-MA) has argued that the OCC's crypto charter approvals, including Coinbase's, are illegal, and the Independent Community Bankers of America has sued the OCC in an effort to stop crypto firms from entering banking through what it describes as a side door.

#5128. Binance to Close Accounts in Four EU Countries Dec. 31, Charging Idle Balances From Nov. 29

Binance will close customer accounts in France, Spain, Italy and Poland on Dec. 31 and begin charging a monthly fee on leftover balances from Nov. 29, as the world’s largest crypto exchange still lacks a license under the European Union’s MiCA rules.

A Binance spokesperson confirmed to CoinDesk the closure date over the weekend, saying the exchange is “progressively restricting certain services for affected users” now that the transition period under the Markets in Crypto-Assets framework has ended.

“Affected accounts will be closed with effect from 31 December 2026,” the spokesperson said, calling it “a carefully managed process.” Binance said customer assets remain safe and accessible and described the move as “no sudden freeze.”

Deposits are already blocked, but users can still withdraw crypto and fiat. Users in the four countries have shared the notices on X.

According to emails that users shared, Binance will charge 0.025% of remaining crypto balances each month, or 10 USDC, whichever is higher, starting Nov. 29. The first charge lands within three days of that date, and a second follows after Dec. 24.

Fees come out of USDC first and then other assets. Accounts holding less than the fee would lose the entire balance, the emails say. Anyone who withdraws everything before Nov. 29 pays nothing.

Binance had not published those fee figures in a public announcement as of Sunday, so the details rest on the customer emails, not an official notice. The company’s spokesperson confirmed only the closure date and the restriction of services.

The four countries share one trait: Binance ran locally registered businesses in each before the MiCA transition ended. Its first outreach to users there came in June, when it urged them to withdraw.

MiCA requires crypto firms to win authorization in one EU member state before serving customers across the bloc. Binance withdrew its Greek license application in June and said it would seek approval elsewhere, without naming the country.

Reuters had reported that month that Greece’s Hellenic Capital Market Commission was poised to reject the bid, as Bitcoin Magazine reported. As the MiCA deadline arrived, the EU’s securities regulator also ordered unlicensed crypto firms to exit the market.

Binance told users at the time that it remained committed to Europe. It does not appear on the European Securities and Markets Authority’s register of authorized crypto providers, and it has not said which regulator now holds its application.

The closures arrive in a rough stretch for the exchange. Hackers stole about $6.3 billion from Binance on Oct. 29 in a record heist the company said began with malware hidden in software downloads and fake job offers targeting engineers. Binance has said it will fully reimburse affected customers, and U.S. banking regulators have since quizzed banks following the hack.

On Saturday, Binance also said finance chief Kaiser Ng is leaving after more than five years. Co-CEO Yi He said in a statement that the transition was planned over the last half year, and Justin Yek, most recently a finance vice president at Airwallex, takes over as CFO.

The FBI has blamed a North Korean hacking group for the theft, which Pyongyang has denied.

It is not clear how many Binance users are affected, and there is no sign so far that accounts in Germany or other EU countries are being closed. Notices cited by French media said accounts still holding assets after Dec. 31 would be treated as inactive.

Rivals that already hold MiCA licenses, including Coinbase and Kraken, stand to gain if Binance exits, as noted in June. The test for Binance is whether it can secure a license before more of its European business is wound down.

Earlier this year, Coinbase also partnered with payments firm Moov to bring stablecoin acceptance, settlement, and real-time funding capabilities to systems used by more than 1,000 community banks and credit unions using Coinbase's Payments API and custodial wallets.

#5125. Microsoft-Backed Space and Time Launches Platform to Let AI Agents Verify Private Data

Microsoft-Backed Space and Time Launches Platform to Let AI Agents Verify Private Data

Space and Time, a Microsoft-backed blockchain data project, has launched a platform designed to let businesses use private data in AI tools without handing over the underlying records, the company announced on Monday.

Space and Time Enterprise lets companies prove that calculations made on their private data are correct, so that AI applications, auditors and other stakeholders can check the results without accessing the database itself. Launch partners include onchain asset manager Maple Finance, Indonesian vehicle maker and distributor Indomobil, Oracle software consultancy Trenez and AI data network The Data Foundation.

"Businesses want AI agents to execute workflows using that information, but those agents don't need unrestricted access to the records behind every calculation," Nate Holiday, CEO of MakeInfinite Labs, the original developer of Space and Time, told Decrypt.

The stakes are high, he argued. "The potential cost to a business is the overall value of the business," Holiday said. "Giving AI tools unrestricted access to data creates another avenue of exposure."

Holiday said the company is working with customers in industries including construction, engineering and manufacturing that want to rebuild their systems around AI, so that agents, reporting tools and compliance teams can work from the same business records and check the calculations they rely on.

The platform is built on Proof of SQL, Space and Time's patented technology for proving that database queries were computed correctly. It already works with Google Cloud's BigQuery, and businesses can now run it on their own servers without operating a blockchain, buying specialized hardware or relying on an outside proving service, according to the company.

With GPU acceleration, supported queries over millions of rows can be run and proven in under a second, Holiday said, though actual performance depends on the query, hardware and dataset.

The technology proves that a calculation was carried out correctly on a given set of records, but it cannot tell whether those records were accurate to begin with. "An incorrect entry still needs to be caught through the business's data controls," Holiday said, "but verification gives those controls a much stronger foundation: evidence of which data was used and whether it was processed correctly."

Space and Time raised $20 million in 2022 in a strategic funding round led by M12, Microsoft's venture fund. Last year, Grayscale launched a trust giving investors exposure to SXT, the token that powers Space and Time's blockchain. Earlier this year, the company launched Dreamspace, an AI-powered tool built with Microsoft's Azure AI services that lets users create and deploy onchain apps without writing code.

Space and Time also runs SXT Chain, a decentralized network that supplies verifiable data to DeFi, tokenized assets and stablecoins. Asked whether enterprise customers would create demand for SXT, Holiday said businesses could deploy the product in their own environment, and that the network would come into play as their applications "need to connect to onchain markets," with those workloads paid for through Space and Time.

The company's cryptography is also used elsewhere. Microsoft's Nova project includes Blitzar, Space and Time's cryptographic acceleration library, and the team's work on a commitment scheme called Dory contributed to a16z crypto's Jolt proof system, Holiday said.

#5124. Mercado Bitcoin begins post-hack reopening without a timeline for withdrawals

Customers can view their pre-hack balances as access returns in phases. The exchange has yet to say when withdrawals will resume.

Crypto exchange Mercado Bitcoin began restoring read-only access to customer accounts in phases on Monday, a week after attackers drained an estimated $49 million from the platform. The exchange has yet to say when customers will be able to withdraw funds.

A Mercado Bitcoin spokesperson told Cointelegraph that access would begin at 19:00 UTC, with all accounts expected to be viewable by 21:00 UTC. Customers will be able to see their holdings as they stood on Nov. 2, the day of the hack.

In a video posted to the exchange’s X account, Fabricio Tota, vice president of new business, said Mercado Bitcoin hoped read-only access would reassure customers that their assets were still recorded on the platform.

Mercado Bitcoin, one of Brazil’s largest crypto exchanges, told Brazilian magazine Veja in late October that it had more than 4.8 million customers and had processed over 215 billion Brazilian reais ($39 billion) in cumulative transactions. The exchange says it is using its own funds to replace the stolen assets, but has yet to give a date for withdrawals to resume.

Tota explained that the company opted for “a more conservative approach” to reactivating accounts “to ensure our return happens with the utmost security.” The caution follows concerns from blockchain security analysts that the attackers were able to access addresses, draining funds through standard transfers rather than a smart contract exploit.

Blockchain analysts traced some of the stolen funds to customer deposit addresses, but Mercado Bitcoin says customers won’t bear the losses. “The vast majority of assets, in fact, nearly all of them, were never even touched by anyone; they were not even involved in the incident,” Tota said in the video.

On Nov. 4, Mercado Bitcoin pledged to cover all losses with company funds, bolstered by a $20 million investment from stablecoin issuer Tether in July. Tota highlighted that a portion of the stolen funds were also frozen in the hours after the company announced an attack had taken place.

The next phase of recovery will require users to confirm their registration details on the platform before they can move funds. The exchange plans to restore withdrawals via Pix, Brazil’s instant payment system, initially allowing customers to withdraw only to accounts in their own name. Tota gave no date for either step.

Mercado Bitcoin has yet to explain how the attackers gained access to its wallets. Blockchain data company Bitquery found that as of Nov. 4, wallets it linked to the attacker held most of the stolen funds that had escaped freezes, including 106.07 BTC.

#5122. StoneX sees 97% upside for Bullish as $4.2 billion Equiniti deal opens tokenization market

Get The Block’s latest crypto news and price alerts for the coins you follow, right in Chrome. Add our free extension here.

StoneX says Bullish's pending $4.2 billion Equiniti acquisition will make it transfer agent of record for about 30% of the S&P 500 and half the FTSE 100.

StoneX Financial on Monday initiated coverage of Bullish with a Buy rating and a $42 price target, arguing that the exchange is set to become a "toll booth" for tokenized shares if its Equiniti acquisition deal follows through.

Bullish shares were trading above $22 in early morning trading, up 2.9% on the day, according to The Block's BLSH price page. StoneX's target implies roughly 97% upside from that level, although the stock is down nearly 50% over six months.

In a note to clients, StoneX analyst Mark Palmer said the Equiniti acquisition will give Bullish a transfer agency business serving nearly 3,000 issuers, 15,000 corporate clients, and 20 million shareholders once completed.

The $4.2 billion Equiniti deal includes $1.85 billion in assumed debt and about $2.35 billion in Bullish shares. Equiniti processes $500 billion in annual payments, according to the note.

On revenue, the exchange's existing business provides a solid base, Palmer noted. Bullish's second-quarter adjusted revenue rose 62% year over year to $92.6 million. Its subscription, services and other revenue also reached a record $62.7 million, per the note.

Meanwhile, Bullish appointed former New York Stock Exchange President Stacey Cunningham as its president. The appointment comes as the company awaits regulatory approval for its Equiniti acquisition and expands its issuer-sponsored tokenization business across Solana, Aptos, and Ethereum.

#5121. BlackRock: Security Fears Are Driving Bitcoin Whales Into IBIT

Large bitcoin holders are swapping coins they guard themselves for shares of BlackRock’s iShares Bitcoin Trust (IBIT) at a record pace, the firm’s head of digital assets said Monday, as kidnappings and hacks push security to the top of their list.

“The current moment is the highest we’ve seen at any point,” Robbie Mitchnick told Barron’s, adding that demand for the swaps “has sort of been one way — up.” Of all the reasons clients give, he said, “the single largest one is security and peace of mind.”

The trades are in-kind conversions. A holder delivers bitcoin to the fund through an intermediary and receives newly created IBIT shares, without first selling the coins for cash. BlackRock relies on Galaxy Digital, Talos and Virtu to handle the transactions, and those firms set the minimum size.

Barron’s put the volume BlackRock has seen at $3.6 billion, noting the real figure could be larger because the firm does not process every conversion itself. Mitchnick cited a bigger number in August, telling Bloomberg that IBIT had facilitated more than $5 billion of such swaps, up from more than $3 billion in October 2025.

In that August interview, BlackRock’s Mitchnick named the threats outright: “People see things happen in the outside world — whether it’s kidnappings, ransom, custody failures — that motivate them to make this switch for all or some of their holdings.”

He told Barron’s the recent Binance hack was not news to big holders. “Most large Bitcoin holders were already acutely aware of the risk of holding on an exchange and in a hot wallet,” he said.

The SEC voted on July 29, 2025, to let spot bitcoin and ether funds create and redeem shares in kind, ending the cash-only structure it required when the funds launched in January 2024.

Access has widened since. BlackRock’s minimum started around $30 million and now hovers between $1 million and $2 million, Barron’s reported.

Taxes are part of the pitch. Converting appreciated bitcoin into IBIT in kind “generally isn’t seen as a sale that would trigger capital-gains taxes,” Barron’s wrote, while Bloomberg cautioned that avoiding an immediate bill depends on the investor’s circumstances. Mitchnick also pointed to estate planning, calling it “much, much simpler” to hold the exposure “within the confines of their private bank.”

Once inside, the coins sit with IBIT’s custodian, an affiliate of Coinbase Prime, in what Mitchnick called an “institutional-grade cold-storage model.” The fund held 795,921 BTC, worth about $60.4 billion, as of Nov. 6.

An in-kind creation adds IBIT shares and counts toward the fund’s reported flows, but no bitcoin is bought on the open market. The coins already existed and simply changed wrappers, so headline flow figures overstate new cash.

Cash has been moving the other way. U.S. spot bitcoin ETFs lost about $1.28 billion in the week ended Nov. 6, their worst week since June, and IBIT accounted for about $1.21 billion of that, Farside Investors data shows.

The fears behind the trade are documented. French authorities have recorded 77 crypto-linked kidnappings, extortion cases and home invasions so far in 2026, more than in all of 2025, the OCCRP reported, citing the national organized crime prosecutor. Reuters reported last year that Ledger’s co-founder lost a finger after being kidnapped with his wife.

The U.S. has its own cases, including a failed Connecticut plot to force a family to hand over bitcoin. Bitcoin security researcher Jameson Lopp maintains a public list of physical attacks on holders dating back to 2014, and the Coldcard bug in August put hardware wallets under scrutiny too.

The shift cuts against bitcoin’s founding pitch of holding money without a bank. Holders who convert give up their private keys for a brokerage claim, trading the risk of guarding coins themselves for reliance on BlackRock, its custodian and the market makers in between.

#5117. Ethereum Foundation, Vitalik Buterin sued over firing after ‘suicide pledge’ dispute

Get The Block’s latest crypto news and price alerts for the coins you follow, right in Chrome. Add our free extension here.

The complaint challenges Fulton’s dismissal and quotes a recorded Buterin conversation about handling internal dissent.

A former Ethereum Foundation employee is suing the organization and members of its leadership, alleging she was unlawfully terminated after challenging an employee mandate.

Kayleen Fulton, known by the moniker "Nixo," filed the complaint on Nov. 4 in Colorado. The defendants include the Ethereum Foundation, its Singapore affiliate, executive Bastian Aue and Ethereum co-founder Vitalik Buterin.

The basis of the lawsuit stems from Fulton's dismissal on April 6 and a dispute over the Foundation's "EF Mandate" that outlines its updated mission and principles. Fulton's suit also alleges disability discrimination and interference with medical leave rights coinciding with a cancer diagnosis she disclosed to the EF around the same time.

The Block reached out to Fulton and the Ethereum Foundation, but did not receive a reply from either before publication.

According to the complaint, employees were given the choice to affirm their commitment to the mandate's direction or leave. However, the filing acknowledges that Aue said the seppuku reference was "not meant to be taken literally" and later clarified that the commitment had "nothing to do with literal seppuku."

Fulton and another colleague, Trent Van Epps, organized a letter endorsing the EF's direction while objecting to some of the conditions of the mandate, which ultimately garnered 53 signatures. The complaint alleges that because of this pushback, Fulton and Van Epps were unlawfully terminated.

The filing also quotes a May 1 conversation with Buterin that Fulton partially recorded. It does not state whether Buterin knew or consented to the conversation being recorded, but notes that it took place in Norway, where one-party consent to recording is legal.

The complaint quotes Buterin as saying that "if you reward making a stink with outcomes," employees could use that as a way to influence the Foundation.

Fulton said she was offered a year's salary under a separation agreement, but alleges she signed the release under duress, received no payments, and later rescinded it.

She is seeking an unspecified amount in damages and lost pay.

#5115. TD Cowen Gives Stablecoin Yield Ban 10% Odds as Banks Plan Push in Democrat-Led Congress

Banks will try again to stop crypto platforms from paying yield on stablecoin balances, but TD Cowen’s Washington analyst Jaret Seiberg puts only a 10% chance on Congress enacting such a ban over the next two years, he wrote in a Monday policy note shared with Bitcoin Magazine and clients.

The issue is whether exchanges and other platforms can offer yield or rewards to investors who keep dollar-pegged stablecoins on their platforms. Seiberg views action as unlikely, though he sees a “narrow path” for a ban.

His rationale is procedural as much as political: it may be easier to find 60 Senate votes on stablecoin yield alone than on the broader crypto market structure bill, which failed a cloture vote 49-50 on Sept. 15 and which he already called effectively dead for this Congress after the Democratic midterm sweep.

A ban, he wrote, would benefit banks. He expects them to renew the push in the next Congress on the theory that Democratic wins open a path.

That path rests on a shift in the Senate. Key crypto advocates retired or lost, while skeptics are being elevated. Sen. Elizabeth Warren is likely to chair the Banking Committee, and Sen. Sherrod Brown, a longtime critic of the industry, regained his Ohio seat after crypto-backed spending of about $64 million failed to stop him, Seiberg wrote before the votes were counted.

It is not his base case. Crypto politics are “complex,” he wrote, which is why the probability starts at 10%.

Some Democrats will resist treating stablecoin yield outside a broader package. Seiberg again sees no path for the Clarity Act, arguing Democrats will insist on ethics and investor-protection provisions President Trump would reject. That split, he wrote, makes Democrats unlikely to unite behind a yield-only bill.

Democrats are not united on the substance either. Sen. Angela Alsobrooks, a lead Democrat on crypto policy, has backed paying rewards for using stablecoins rather than for passively holding them, a distinction that shaped the Tillis-Alsobrooks compromise text during Clarity talks.

There is also a perception problem. Congress would be asked to stop voters from earning a return on stablecoin holdings even though they can earn one on savings accounts and money market funds. Seiberg questions whether Democratic leaders want a vote that could be framed as taking money from retail investors.

The fight is leftover business from the GENIUS Act, which President Trump signed on July 18, 2025, as Public Law 119-27. The statute bars permitted and foreign payment stablecoin issuers from paying “any form of interest or yield” solely for holding, using or retaining a payment stablecoin.

It does not explicitly cover exchanges and other intermediaries. That gap is what banks call the loophole. The American Bankers Association and state bankers associations urged Congress late last year to treat platform rewards as banned interest, and the ABA and Independent Community Bankers of America later pressed the Senate to harden Clarity language against balance- and tenure-based rewards. ICBA has warned that yield-bearing payment stablecoins could pull deposits from community banks and cut local lending.

Stablecoins are the main trading pair for bitcoin on most exchanges, so the yield fight lands on how bitcoin markets settle and how platforms attract dollar balances. The OCC’s final GENIUS rules, effective in January 2027, left the third-party rewards question for case-by-case review after banks had hoped the agency would close it.

House Democrats are already sketching their own market structure approach with a bigger SEC role. Seiberg’s note treats a narrow yield ban as the one bank ask that might still clear 60 votes even if Clarity does not.

The reason the odds are not zero, he wrote, is that community banks are effective at arguing that stablecoin yields pull funds out of banks and hurt local lending, and small banks have a record of getting Congress to act. He remains dubious.

The test for the next Congress is whether banks can peel yield off the broader crypto fight, or whether Democratic splits and the retail-investor optics keep the probability where Seiberg put it Monday: one in ten.

#5114. Formation Securities plans Solana stock trading venue under SEC exemption

The proposed venue would offer 24/7 trading of tokenized shares from Galaxy Digital, Exodus and Forward Industries, with a launch planned for early 2027.

Formation Securities, a newly established company licensing technology from decentralized exchange Orca, has notified the US Securities and Exchange Commission (SEC) of plans to launch a tokenized stock trading venue on Solana in early 2027.

The company said Monday that tokenized shares of Galaxy Digital, Exodus Movement and Forward Industries are expected to be available at launch. The venue would operate under the SEC’s temporary Innovation Exemption announced in September, which allows verified investors to trade certain US-listed shares around the clock while retaining voting, dividend and other shareholder rights.

Superstate and Securitize are expected to make tokenized shares available on the marketplace, which Formation says it intends to develop in cooperation with public companies. The venue would use automated market maker (AMM) technology, allowing investors to hold securities in their own wallets and settle trades onchain against stablecoins and other eligible assets.

Traditional transfer agents would continue to maintain official shareholder records, preserving companies’ direct relationships with shareholders.

Solana is already among the largest blockchains for tokenized equities, with approximately $575 million in tokenized stock value as of Monday, compared with $747 million on Ethereum and $1.7 billion on BNB Chain, according to RWA.xyz data.

Related: Robinhood’s Tenev warns US tokenization uncertainty deters ‘serious assets’

Formation’s issuer-focused approach comes amid growing debate over how publicly traded shares should be brought onchain. As of early November, more than 50 companies had objected to having their shares tokenized on OKXICE, a proposed venue backed by crypto exchange OKX and New York Stock Exchange parent Intercontinental Exchange.

Under the SEC’s Innovation Exemption, trading venues must notify issuers in writing and wait at least 30 calendar days before trading shares tokenized by unaffiliated third parties, giving companies an opportunity to object. Nasdaq warned in September that tokenized stock trading could have unforeseen consequences for liquidity, price discovery and long-term shareholder value.

#5113. NYSE Taps Avalanche to Settle Its Planned Tokenized Stock Platform

NYSE Taps Avalanche to Settle Its Planned Tokenized Stock Platform

The New York Stock Exchange is turning to Avalanche to power the behind-the-scenes plumbing of its forthcoming tokenized securities platform, one of the clearest signs yet that Wall Street’s oldest institutions are moving core market infrastructure onto public blockchains.

Ava Labs, the company behind Avalanche, said Monday that the blockchain will serve as the “core ledger technology” for the clearing firm behind an NYSE-affiliated digital trading venue.

The platform, which the NYSE’s parent Intercontinental Exchange previously said it was developing, is designed to allow 24/7 trading, clearing and settlement of tokenized securities, subject to regulatory approval. Running on Avalanche, it aims to bring the clearing of tokenized equities onchain at institutional scale.

Tokenization, the practice of issuing traditional assets as blockchain-based tokens, has become one of Wall Street’s buzziest pursuits, but most efforts so far have centered on individual products rather than the market machinery itself.

The NYSE’s move is notable because it puts a blockchain underneath the clearing and settlement layer, the plumbing that finalizes trades, rather than just wrapping a single fund or stock in a token.

Dennis Lee, the NYSE’s head of digital issuance, said the exchange is “picking the right technology for each job,” and that while its planned platform will support equities listed across a range of blockchains, the ledger recording and settling trades needs to be fast, reliable and able to control who participates.

Emin Gün Sirer, Ava Labs’ CEO and founder, framed the deal as proof that blockchains can support not just products but the infrastructure they trade on, citing Avalanche’s “24/7 atomic settlement” and predictable costs.

The announcement adds to a frenetic race to put stocks and their underlying infrastructure onchain.

The NYSE separately tapped Blockchain.com to reach crypto investors with tokenized stocks and ETFs, while ICE and crypto exchange OKX are planning a 24/7 tokenized stock venue under an SEC exemption, and Morgan Stanley has moved to tokenize securities.

That OKX-ICE effort has hit friction, though: Every Magnificent Seven stock, including Apple and Meta, opted out of having its shares tokenized on the venue, with 67 companies in all joining them.

Research shows the appetite for tokenized stocks is real but uneven. An IMF study found tokenized stocks show genuine demand yet remain volatile and illiquid, a reminder that the infrastructure may be arriving faster than the markets to fill it.
