#5013. The World’s Most Famous Architects Are Building in Albania. I Had to Find Out Why

“I’ll try to focus on this, which is not the easiest thing today,” says Edi Rama, the embattled prime minister of Albania, after a night spent monitoring violent protests in the capital, Tirana. But Rama quickly warms to one of his favorite subjects. “Architecture,” he says, choosing his words carefully, “has the power to change societies. It’s like a strong medicine. It can heal, or it can kill.”

As a journalist who covers architecture, I had been hearing about a building boom in Albania for several years. It seemed like half the architects I knew in New York were on a first-name basis with the prime minister, a working artist and, at six-foot-seven, a former pro basketball player. So on October 19 I flew to Tirana—a short side trip from Greece, where I was on vacation with my husband—to look at some of the new buildings and interview Rama about them. But I arrived during a season of civil unrest. The prime minister, whose assistant had told me I needn’t postpone the trip because everything was fine, had moved to a secure location on the outskirts of the city; some of the buildings I had come to see had just been vandalized by protesters; and at one point I was accused, publicly, of aiding a government spy. (The charge was—need I even say this—absurd.) And, because I’m in the early stages of Parkinson’s disease, I experienced all this from a four-wheeled mobility scooter, which I had rented for about $100.

As I tooled around the city center, much of it seemed unaffected by the protests. In fact, most of Tirana was charming. But weren’t those Molotov cocktails we heard going off during an alfresco dinner? Those weren’t on my drinks menu.

What was on my menu in Tirana, a city of about 600,000 in a country of about 2.3 million, was confusion. Confusion about why the protesters were so angry, and about why the prime minister seemed so unconcerned. In his 13 years in office, Rama, 62, has been remaking as much of Albania as possible with the help of foreign architects. Even the heavily guarded villa where he agreed to be interviewed, a raw concrete building dyed bright red, is largely the work of the Spanish architect Pablo Bofill.

When protesters began demanding Rama’s resignation last May, he was about to host his second annual gathering of architects from around the world, one of the high points of his year. Architects like the Danish superstar Bjarke Ingels and the Chilean Alejandro Aravena, who won the Pritzker Prize in 2016, “pay to come here and talk, and they love it,” Rama says. But this year the get-together, from June 3 to 5, drew protesters with signs that read “Starchitects go home!” Those protests presaged the more violent demonstrations in October and November.

I haven’t attended either of Rama’s conclaves, but what I’ve heard reminds me of my visit to Ordos City, Inner Mongolia, in 2008, which I wrote about for The New York Times. A wealthy industrialist, aided by the artist Ai Weiwei, had brought 100 young architects from around the world to the isolated desert city. Their task was to design 100 villas for anonymous (and seemingly nonexistent) Chinese millionaires. Both situations felt surreal.

Rama normally works in an office on the Boulevard of Martyrs of the Nation in the center of Tirana. But on October 20 the boulevard’s sidewalks were missing hundreds of paving stones, which protesters had used to shatter storefronts. And some of the stones were reportedly thrown at police during nighttime clashes that authorities say left hundreds injured. Police have used tear gas and rubber bullets as protesters set small buildings, like an information kiosk in the vast Skanderbeg Square, on fire. Near the shell of the kiosk, workers scrubbed fresh graffiti off a statue of Skanderbeg himself. The Tirana Pyramid, built in the late 1980s by followers of the brutal dictator Enver Hoxha, and recently turned into a public amenity by the Rotterdam firm MVRDV, was also damaged.

There’s a reason some Albanians—it’s impossible to say how many—resent the foreign architects and the attention lavished on them. In the 26 years since he became the mayor of Tirana and then prime minister, Rama has initiated or encouraged more than 50 international architecture competitions for projects ranging from parks to museums to large housing developments. Forty-eight of those were won by foreign architects (some working with Albanian firms), according to Adelina Greca, who leads the National Territorial Planning Agency, which organizes competitions for the government. Only two were won by Albanian firms working alone. In addition, Rama has played matchmaker, giving private developers the names of foreign architects he strongly recommends they hire.

As a result, Tirana’s compact city center is filled with buildings in bright colors and novel shapes. There is a so-called vertical forest, a skyscraper laden with trees and shrubs, by the Italian architect Stefano Boeri. The green-colored building is just a stone’s throw from the bright red Marriott by another Italian architect, Marco Casamonti. The Hotel Plaza Tirana, by architect Johan Anrys, of the Belgian firm 51N4E, starts as an ellipse at its base and morphs into a rectangle at its crown. But the champion attention-getter is a condo building by MVRDV in the shape of a head. Its façade and balconies resolve into facial features, most conspicuously a pronounced nose. The architects have publicly identified it as the head of Skanderbeg, a 15th-century military hero. But some Albanians think it’s Rama. Both ideas are “urban legends,” says the prime minister. “It’s the form of a man through history,” he says, contradicting the architects’ own statement. Nodding at this reporter, he says, “It also looks like you.”

Tirana’s Rock (a.k.a. the Skanderbeg building) is an MVRDV-designed condo in the shape of a head

A Marriott, designed by Marco Casamonti of Archea Associati, left, and the “vertical forest” skyscraper, by Stefano Boeri

The vertical forest, the Marriott, and the Plaza Hotel are all handsome additions to the city. So, too, is the “head,” but is it too much of an architectural one-liner? Who, I wonder as I pass it several times a day, is going to want to live in the nose?

Johan Anrys, who 22 years ago became one of the first foreign architects in Albania, told me that working there has taught him “what architecture truly means for improving society.” He said he knows that Albanian architects wonder, Are we not good enough? and believe we’re taking jobs from them. “It isn’t true. I have a studio in Tirana with 20 Albanian architects.” Overall he says, the foreign architects have “brought Albanian architects more work than we’ve taken.”

Rama tells me—on-the-record, and perhaps unwisely—that the only Albanian architects who have a problem with his overtures to foreign firms are “the mediocre ones.” The rest, he explains, see that having first-rate architects from abroad, including Herzog & de Meuron, OMA, and Diller Scofidio & Renfro, working in Albania will raise everyone’s game.

Rama tells me—on-the-record, and perhaps unwisely—that the only Albanian architects who have a problem with his overtures to foreign firms are “the mediocre ones.”

The foreign architects who win competitions are required to enlist architects licensed in Albania to help them execute their plans. Rama compares the situation to that of China, which 20 years ago was giving its most prominent building commissions to foreign architects, including some of the same ones working in Albania today. Chinese architects, Rama says, got a free education from the western masters.

“If we continue this kind of cooperation,” says Greca, who has worked for Rama almost continuously since 2003, “there will come a day when Albanian architects will win competitions not only here but around the world.”

Anti-government protesters on the streets of Tirana, with the art installation Tirana Moons by Austrian architect Chris Precht in the background

But Greca’s association with Rama has made her a controversial figure among local architects. On my first day in town, our route intersects that of a lunchtime protest march. We stop to watch. Greca notices many architects and architecture students in the crowd. (The Faculty of Architecture and Urbanism (FAU) of the Polytechnic University of Tirana is nearby.)

Within minutes, Gent Progni, an organizer of the march, posts a photo of Greca and me on Instagram. The caption claims she had sunk so low as to use a disabled person (me) as cover to spy on the marchers. (Ten days later, the post has almost 7,000 “likes.”) Later, in a text responding to questions from this reporter, Progni wrote: “My opposition is not to foreign architects but to the way Edi Rama uses prestigious international names to legitimize controversial development projects, often without transparency, meaningful public consultation, or accountability. This is a significant issue for our protest movement, but it is part of a much larger struggle against corruption, the concentration of power, and the sale of Albania’s public wealth.”

Rama himself isn’t happy with all of the new buildings in Tirana. He complained about one whose façades are made of stone “until a certain altitude,” where the cost-cutting developer switched to a printed material that only looks like stone. Other buildings stand partially complete, or complete but eerily empty. And in several cases developers have replaced foreign architects with local ones midstream, in order to complete buildings without meeting the original design specifications. “We need the investors to stick with the architects,” Rama says. But funding is less available than he would like. “Certain architecture needs a certain quality,” he says, philosophically. “But compromises are forced by the limitations of the place.”

The problems with his building boom go deeper than façade materials. There are accusations of money laundering and other forms of corruption, which grew louder at the end of May, when Rama agreed to support efforts by a company associated with Jared Kushner and Ivanka Trump to build luxury resorts on previously undeveloped land. It was a coincidence that the big architecture conference started just a few days later, leading directly to the calls for starchitects to scram. Among the protesters, that demand became entangled with environmental and anti-Trump sentiments in what became known as the Flamingo Revolution.

A housing project by Madrid-based firm SelgasCano

European journalist and curator Anneke Abhelakh has recently published a brilliantly illuminating book, The Albanian Files, which gives 60 foreign architecture firms eight or more pages to describe the work they are doing in Albania. Altogether, the book lists more than 500 projects. Of those, only about one-tenth have been realized. Rama says he is looking forward to the completion of many more.

But can that really happen, given the pressure to resign? The prime minister, who won the most recent election by the biggest majority of his career, says he isn’t going anywhere.

In fact, seemingly oblivious to the mood on the street, he wants to further celebrate his stable of foreign architects. Near the end of my half-hour interview, he mentions that he is thinking of building a museum modeled after The Albanian Files, with one room for each of the 60 foreign firms to show drawings, models, and photographs of their work. He said he already has a site for what he says will be “a big store of dreams, hopes and challenges, illusions and delusions. It will be a great place to go.” Its designer will be chosen, naturally, in an international architectural competition.

I left Albania with lots of questions and thousands of photos to sort through. In one of them I have my arm around Rama. Why the show of affection? A reflexive pose when the camera comes out. But I suppose that, politics aside, I can’t hate a man who loves architecture.

Prime Minster Rama with writer Fred Bernstein

#5012. Jessica McClain's Approach to Running Breaks a Lot of the Usual Rules. It's Working.

The Brooks athlete spoke to Women’s Health fresh off the NYC Marathon.

After a major marathon, you hear about two different podiums. There’s the actual podium, made up of the top three finishers, and then another, unofficial one of the fastest Americans. That’s because, though there’s some incredible talent in the U.S., oftentimes, it’s simply not enough to beat out international runners, including on our own courses. In the New York City Marathon women’s race, for example, an American hasn’t been in the top three for eight years. (In the men’s race, it’s been since 2009.)

That streak ended this Sunday when Brooks athlete Jessica McClain crossed the finish line in 2:24:42, coming in at second place. Even more exciting: She was poised to get third but overtook last year’s winner Hellen Obiri in the final meters. She did it looking effortless, with a pair of Goodr sunglasses in her hand and a huge smile on her face.

Even before the race cannon went off that morning, her momentum had been building. In the spring, McClain placed fifth in the Boston Marathon at 2:20:49 and set an American course record. Going into New York, she initially wanted to be in the top five again. “But then I was like, ‘Wait, I want to do better than I did in Boston, and that's fourth, and I do not want fourth place,’” she tells Women’s Health. So, it was settled: She was shooting for third or higher.

After a performance like she had in New York, you can expect that’s always going to be the goal now for the 34-year-old. “I think ‘top American’ just isn't going to cut it anymore for where I want to be on the international stage,” she says. “I want to compete and I want to practice competing, especially because we're coming up on the Olympic trials, and the top three go to the Olympics. I want to practice competing for those top three spots no matter the race.”

Heading into NYC, McClain was feeling relaxed. The race started out hard, she remembers, so she just focused on getting her fuel in at each aid station and taking the run mile by mile. “I was just checking boxes and staying out of trouble as much as I could the first 13 to 16 miles,” she says. Then, once she got to Manhattan, the pack started to run faster, her gels started to kick in, and she started to feel better. Still, it wasn’t until the last 10K when the running started to feel really good.

McClain was strategic until the end—running the tangents and taking the shortest turns possible to close the gap between Obiri and first-place finisher Sheila Chepkirui, who she noticed were making their turns a little wider and slower. Even down to the last stretch turning into Central Park, second wasn’t exactly on McClain’s mind. She was just thrilled to take third. She took off her sunglasses to take it all in, which is why they were in her hand as she crossed the finish line.

“Then I saw Hellen looking around and was like, ‘Oh my God, wait, I think I can get second,’” she says. So, she dug deep, pumped her arms, and fed off the crowd’s energy. “I just felt really, really good,” she says. “I've run a lot of reps at the end of a lot of hard workouts that were fast and I was like, ‘I can do this.’” The result was one of the most exciting second-place finishes to a marathon in a long time.

Despite her headline-grabbing performances in 2026, it was just a few years ago that McClain stepped away from professional running after hitting what she describes as “rock bottom.” She was chronically injured, having dealt with her share of tight tendons, stress fractures, plantar issues, and strained muscles, and unable to race at her full potential. So, she moved home to Arizona, got a full-time job in the nonprofit sector, and reevaluated her relationship with running.

“It gave me a lot of perspective,” McClain says. “For a long time I was very outcome oriented, not process driven. It was a lot about ‘We need to hit these times and get this place,’ and I wasn't enjoying the ride to get there, and it trickled into a lot of other things in life.”

From 2020 to 2022, McClain just ran for herself. She didn’t have a super formal plan or even a coach. She worked full time, met the man who is now her husband, reconnected with old friends, and had fun outside of her sport. “I needed that, but naturally found my way back [to running],” she says. She signed up for the Mesa Marathon in February 2022 to celebrate her 30th birthday—and won it.

Slowly, she started racing again, even self-coaching her way to a fourth-place performance in the 2024 Olympic marathon trials. Now, she’s resigned with Brooks and back to racing as a professional, but she still maintains all those other parts of herself she gained in her time off. That period away from professional running didn’t sabotage her progress. Instead, it set her up for this latest era, one where she’s happier—and more successful—than ever.

Stepping away from professional running led to a mindset shift for McClain. First, she prioritizes fun—and you can tell. Obviously, coming off of an incredible finish at one of the world’s biggest marathons will put you in a good mood, but it’s clear that McClain is enthusiastic about running. She leads with such a pure love for the sport that pros (and amateurs) can lose sight of, that she had once lost sight of until her time away.

“This is supposed to be fun,” McClain says. “Running is amazing, but at the end of the day, it's just running and there's so much else in life. I just want to enjoy it and to get the best out of myself and make the sport a little bit better, whatever that means.”

That philosophy carries over into her day-to-day training, where she prioritizes workouts she actually enjoys. “Eighty-five percent of the work I do gives me confidence, and then there's 15 percent that's really hard that I don't love, but that's good for me,” McClain says. She’s also not a cross-trainer. “It does not bring me joy,” McClain says. “I hate swimming. I hate biking.” Yes, even if she gets injured. When it comes to strength training, she keeps that minimal too.

Similarly, you’ll rarely catch McClain wrapping up a session doubled over and exhausted, she says. “I’m never hitting a home run in practice,” she says. “That’s for racing.”

Due to her full-time job as the executive director at the Johnjay & Rich #LoveUp Foundation, her weekly mileage also leans a little shorter than some of her competitors. (During this training block, McClain ran about 72 to 84 miles every week, with one hard session and one long run per week in addition to her easy miles.) Her coach, David Roche, also doesn’t assign runs based on paces. Instead, they focus on effort.

Beyond keeping training from getting too draining, McClain has seen another incredible benefit from this approach: She’s been injury-free for nearly four years. “That's something that just gives me a little bit of an edge,” she says. “I've had a lot of healthy blocks strung together.”

When it comes to nutrition, McClain takes the same refreshingly uncomplicated approach, and it’s paying off. “I just feel like I’m the strongest athlete I’ve ever been,” she says. It’s all very intuitive when it comes to deciding what to eat. You won’t catch her planning out meals or counting macros. There’s nothing super strict or specific to her diet. She eats what she wants and relies on snacks to keep her well-fed. “I’m never hungry,” she says.

Plus, her fueling plan doesn’t come in between her and living life. “In the off season and when my season isn't super crazy, I go out with friends, I have fun, I go to happy hour. I don't try to live in restriction or super high monitoring ever,” McClain says. When she gets closer to race day, she dials in a bit more, but since she’s been so relaxed otherwise, it just makes it fun to lock in for a month.

There is one food that’s become a mainstay in her diet: Burgers—two to three nights a week. “It's super easy,” McClain says. “Throw it on the grill, slap it between two buns and put whatever you want on it. It's great and I get a ton of protein.” Her sweet treat of choice is Dairy Queen, a spot she frequents.

Next, McClain has her sights set on the Olympics. She placed fourth at the last trials, making her an alternate for Paris but just missing the chance to race for the country.

In the meantime, as she waits for 2028, she’s going to keep crushing the marathon and competing for every podium. “It's taken a long time to get to the point where I look at these women—and they're women I've been watching and idolizing in the marathon for quite some time—and I'm like, ‘Oh no, I can race with them too,’” she says. But now, there’s no doubting that she can.

Olivia Luppino is an associate editor at Women’s Health. She reports, writes, and edits content ranging from service stories to in-depth features and awards packages across all verticals, including health, fitness, sports, and beauty. While at WH, she’s traveled to Paris, France to watch Faith Kipyegon attempt a sub-4-minute mile and covered major races like Ultra Trail du Mont Blanc in Chamonix. Olivia is a RRCA-certified running coach who is especially passionate about helping people start their running journey and is training for her second marathon. Her works also appears in New York Magazine’s The Cut, PS (formerly Popsugar), and Salon, where she also did on-camera interviews with celebrity guests.

#5011. Bosch’s popular e-bike motor just got upgraded with more power and less weight

Bosch has unveiled the latest generation of its popular Active Line Plus electric bicycle motor, bringing more power, less weight, and a significantly smaller footprint to one of the company’s most widely used drive systems.

The new motor is designed primarily for everyday riders, including commuters, recreational cyclists, and those who want a comfortable e-bike that can handle everything from city streets to weekend trail rides.

And while it isn’t Bosch’s most powerful motor, the upgrades are fairly substantial.

The new Active Line Plus now produces up to 600 watts of peak power and 60 Nm of torque, along with a maximum pedal assistance level of 340%. That’s enough to give riders a healthy boost when accelerating away from traffic lights or climbing hills, without necessarily turning their everyday commuter into an overpowered mountain bike.

Like its predecessor, the motor is designed for Class 1 electric bicycles in the US, providing pedal assistance up to 20 mph (32 km/h) without a throttle.

Remember, the Active Line motor is the drive system model that many e-bike companies use for their modestly performing e-bikes. It gives good power and control, combined with all the tech we’ve come to expect from Bosch, but it isn’t the motor e-bike makers use when they need an electric mountain bike to scale a steep mountainous trail.

Perhaps even more interesting than the increased power is what Bosch has done to the motor’s physical design.

The company developed a new two-stage gearbox that helps reduce the motor’s overall volume by 17% while shaving approximately 1.1 lb (0.5 kg) off its weight.

The new drive unit now tips the scales at around 6 lb (2.7 kg), making it easier for bicycle manufacturers to integrate into slimmer, more traditional-looking frames.

Bosch also says the new gearbox uses specially machined gears to make the motor virtually silent, while updated sensors and software provide smoother, more natural-feeling pedal assistance.

That’s particularly important for a motor like the Active Line Plus, where riders are often looking for an experience that feels like riding a regular bicycle, just with a bit of extra help.

The motor also supports Bosch’s eShift electronic shifting system, which can automatically manage gear changes for riders who prefer to focus on pedaling instead of shifting.

Bosch has additionally improved the motor’s sealing and bearings for better all-weather durability. The housing uses approximately 40% recycled aluminum, helping reduce the carbon footprint of its production.

Trek will be the first North American bicycle brand to introduce the new Active Line Plus motor, installing it on the company’s Marlin+ electric bike.

Bosch also announced several other additions to its e-bike ecosystem, including a new compact LED Controller that’s 65% smaller than its predecessor, an updated ConnectModule with Bluetooth-based theft tracking, and a new 720 Wh PowerTube battery weighing just 8.6 lb (3.9 kg).

There’s also a new Intuvia 200 display, offering a 2.4-inch grayscale screen for riders who prefer a traditional handlebar-mounted display.

While high-performance e-bike motors tend to grab the headlines with increasingly outrageous torque figures, there’s something to be said for improvements to the more practical end of the market – something for the riders looking for convenient and effective transportation instead of peak performance figures.

For the average commuter or recreational rider, a quieter, lighter motor with plenty of usable power is likely to make a bigger difference than squeezing another few dozen Newton-meters out of an already powerful drive system.

And with Bosch’s extensive network of bicycle manufacturing partners, we can expect to see this updated motor making its way into plenty of new electric bikes.

#5010. Anthropic Sets IPO Price Range at $70 to $78, Valuing Company at Up to $1.4 Trillion

Anthropic Sets IPO Price Range at $70 to $78, Valuing Company at Up to $1.4 Trillion

Anthropic has set a price range of $70 to $78 per share for its initial public offering, according to an updated filing with the Securities and Exchange Commission on Tuesday.

The AI company plans to sell 825 million shares of Class A common stock, 801.4 million of them new shares and 23.6 million from existing shareholders, on the Nasdaq under the ticker "ANT," which it revealed when it made its IPO filing public last month. At the top of the range, the offering would raise about $64.4 billion, including the selling shareholders' stake. Underwriters have an option to buy a further 123.75 million shares.

Anthropic expects to price the offering on November 18 and begin trading on November 19, and is targeting a retail allocation of about 20%, well above the norm for an IPO, CNBC reported, citing people familiar with the details.

Based on the roughly 13.09 billion shares the filing says will be outstanding after the offering, the range implies a market value of about $916 billion to $1.02 trillion. On a fully diluted basis, the company could be worth up to $1.405 trillion according to CNBC, up from the $965 billion valuation it announced in a funding round in May.

Alongside the IPO, seven of the companies that supply Anthropic's computing power have agreed to buy a combined $26 billion of Class N common stock, which has no voting rights, at the IPO price. At the midpoint of the range, Google and Nvidia would each buy about $10 billion, AMD about $2.5 billion, Broadcom, Micron and Microsoft about $1 billion each, and TSMC about $500 million, according to the filing. Those shares will not be subject to the usual post-IPO lock-up period.

Anthropic expects net proceeds of about $84.6 billion from the IPO and the private placement combined at the midpoint, which it plans to use for general corporate purposes, including investment in compute and infrastructure, according to the filing.

The company reported revenue of $33.2 billion for the first nine months of 2026, up from $2.5 billion a year earlier. It posted a net loss of $104 billion over the period, most of it from a $94.3 billion non-cash charge tied to the rising value of its convertible notes and other instruments; its operating loss was $1.26 billion. Anthropic has also committed to about $900.9 billion of spending, mostly on compute and infrastructure, of which about $70.7 billion is due within the next 12 months.

Anthropic will list as a public benefit corporation with an unusual governance structure. Its Long-Term Benefit Trust, a body of financially disinterested trustees, will elect a majority of its board, while a single share of Class F stock held by an entity owned by its seven co-founders will carry 50.1% of the company's voting power on most matters, according to the filing. As a result, Anthropic will be a "controlled company" under Nasdaq rules, and holders of its Class A shares will have limited influence over major decisions, the filing warns.

The range comes after a turbulent week for AI stocks. Some major investors told The Wall Street Journal last week that Anthropic's expected $1.5 trillion valuation would likely need to come down following a sell-off in AI stocks, with one suggesting a figure closer to $1 trillion. Anthropic had been eyeing a valuation of around $2 trillion as recently as September, according to an earlier draft filing.

The IPO's order book is already covered at the proposed range, CNBC reported, citing people familiar with the matter.

#5008. Yoshitomo Nara Is Gen Z’s Favorite Artist—Even if They Don’t Know His Name

Four years ago, when Terrell Brown was a college student in Wheaton, Illinois, he spotted an oversized ashtray at a local Goodwill. It featured a cartoonlike illustration of a fierce-looking girl smoking a cigarette, along with the words “Too Young to Die.” It cost him $10. Brown recognized the imagery but didn’t know the name of the artist who created it. But when he posted a photo on Reddit, a reader commented, “Yoshitomo Nara is a really cool and famous artist, this is an amazing find!”

Brown, now a vintage reseller with almost 360,000 followers on TikTok, resold the ashtray, still in its original packaging, on eBay for $2,860. The episode says something about the powerful attraction—and afterlife—of Nara’s imagery. The artist’s signature figures—round-headed children with enormous eyes and expressions that oscillate between mischief, defiance, and melancholy—are deceptively simple. They often feature slogans like, “No nukes” and “Rock ’n’ roll is not dead.”

Fans include the world’s wealthiest—like Salesforce founder Marc Benioff, a longtime Yoshitomo Nara collector who supported a major retrospective of Nara’s work last year at London’s Hayward Gallery, co-organized by the Guggenheim Museum Bilbao and Museum Frieder Burda in Baden-Baden. His work has also become something of a fixture in homes featured in AD, from the Nara sculpture in Usher’s backyard to the painting hanging in Kris Jenner’s dining room. But his fans also include Gen Z’ers who love the characters without necessarily knowing much about the artist behind them. Nara’s artwork has attained a kind of cult currency, circulating through dorm rooms, online marketplaces, thrift stores, and social media, the characters becoming so familiar they seem to belong to everyone.

Stella McCartney x Yoshitomo Nara handbag

This week, as powerhouse art dealer David Zwirner unveils the first major solo exhibition of Nara’s work in New York in over a decade (Nara’s first at the gallery since leaving Pace last year), that duality is especially striking. The highly anticipated show is poised to be a museum-quality affair. Curated by Nara himself, it will feature paintings, drawings, and sculptures across the gallery’s two Chelsea locations. Yet the gallery will undoubtedly also draw a crowd of broader Nara fans—the public who adore his instantly recognizable characters, even if they know them best as decorative icons closer to the cultural territory of Hello Kitty or Miffy.

Now in his 60s and based in Tochigi Prefecture, about 100 miles north of Tokyo, Nara emerged on the international art scene in the 1990s. His creations’ simultaneously innocent and world-weary affect is central to their appeal. The characters “look cute on the surface,” says Takako Nagasawa, deputy chairwoman at Phillips auction house, “but if you read into them, they’re not cute, like the girl who has a knife hidden behind her back.” Many collectors, she notes, display Nara’s work in their children’s bedrooms. “I think that two-sidedness is the attraction, and very relevant now. We want to talk about what’s going on around the world, but we also don’t want to talk about it.”

Usher and wife Jennifer Goicoechea Raymond, in front of Yoshitomo Nara’s Peace Head in their garden

Usher’s daughter’s room, which showcases two Nara artworks

Yoshitomo Nara, Matryoshka, 2026. © Yoshitomo Nara 2026, courtesy of the artist, Equivalence Art Agency, and David Zwirner

Yoshitomo Nara painting in a home published in the July 2013 issue of AD

Nara prizes accessibility to his art and has actively participated in extending his imagery into everyday products. While other artists have created characters that appear on merchandise, like Kaws’s Companion and Jeff Koons’s balloon dog, none have endured and proliferated quite like Nara’s, which have an underlying rebelliousness that speaks to disaffected youth across generations. Yoshiki, the frontman of the rock band X Japan, is working with Nara on the packaging for his Napa Valley wines, Y by Yoshiki, adorned with the artist’s characters. Stella McCartney, a close friend of Nara’s, has previewed her third limited-edition collaboration with the artist, ranging from $100 shoelaces to a $7,900 knit scarf. The merchandise Nara designs often connects to themes in his art, like the $12 bandages, $85 snow globes, and $1,200 table lamps available at the MoMA Design Store. (He is one of the most searched artists on MoMA’s website, says Chay Costello, the museum’s associate director of merchandising and product development.)

Sweaters from Stella McCartney’s third collaboration with Yoshitomo Nara

Stella McCartney x Yoshitomo Nara collection will be available starting November 11

Content creator Nick Lowry, who bought one of Nara’s ashtrays 20 years ago on eBay for $250, posted a TikTok video a few months ago of himself removing the object from its longtime spot on his coffee table and hanging it on the wall. (The original acrylic painting on which the ashtray is based is in the collection of the Rubell Museum, in Miami.) “Objects like this are one of the only ways to own work by a living artist whose paintings now sell for more than I could ever afford,” Lowry says in the video, “so it deserved a better spot.”

Benioff describes Nara as “one of the most important artists of our time.” Like many others, Benioff came to Nara’s work through eBay. He purchased journals designed by the artist and his “little books” about the Zen Buddhist concept of Shoshin, commonly translated as “beginner’s mind.” Benioff, who met Nara through Yoshiki, now considers the artist a close friend. He owns multiple artworks by the artist, including a drawing of a grimacing girl that reads “I’m just alright,” a totemic fiberglass sculpture of two heads in a bowl, and a monumental bronze head. “It’s great that I picked them up early,” he says of the pieces. “It’s really almost impossible to get anything [on the primary market]. The best pieces all go to the museums.”

A Yoshitomo Nara painting in a home published in the January 2018 issue of AD

Yoshitomo Nara, The Little Pilgrims (Night Walking), up for auction this week at Sotheby’s

Numerous Nara works are coming to auction in conjunction with the opening of the Zwirner show. (Nara’s auction record is $25 million for his seven-and-a-half-foot canvas Knife Behind Back, sold at Sotheby’s Hong Kong in 2019.) His eight-and-a-half-foot painting Milky Lake / Thinking One, featuring a little girl with downcast eyes, is coming up for sale later this month at the online auction platform Fair Warning at an estimate of $10 million. Next week, Christie’s is offering an eight-foot-high bronze version of his character Miss Forest, for $1 million, in its 21st Century Evening Sale, and Sotheby’s is selling his painting Candy Blue Night, from 2001, at an estimate of $5 million, and a set of fiberglass sculptures, The Little Pilgrims (Night Walking), from a 1999 edition, at an estimate of $1.2 million. (The sellers, Theodore and Ruth Baum, bought the set at auction two decades ago for just $360,000.)

For the Zwirner show, Nara has paired works from his student days in Düsseldorf in the 1980s and 1990s with recent ones, demonstrating how he incorporated drawing into his practice throughout his career. He’s also exhibiting 13 oversized bronze heads, enlarged from palm-sized clay models inspired by the snow “monsters” from his childhood winters in northern rural Japan. The show coincides with the reopening last week of the Hirshhorn Sculpture Garden in Washington, DC, after a four-year renovation, where three larger aluminum versions of the heads are on view.

Nara sculptures at the reopened Hirshhorn Sculpture Garden in Washington, DC

For most artists, the distance between the audiences for Nara’s masterpieces and his mass-produced products would be difficult to bridge. But for Nara, it may be the very thing that makes him so compelling. His work can be both a million-dollar trophy and a teenager’s trinket. And while the Zwirner show draws the art world’s big collectors and curators, the next generation of fans may be discovering him somewhere else entirely: on TikTok, Instagram, or in a gift shop.

Yoshitomo Nara bandages sold at MoMA Design Store

#5007. Decrypt Money Account Gets Confidential Transfers With Solana Privacy Firm Helius

Decrypt is partnering with Solana infrastructure company Helius to bring confidential transfers to the Decrypt Money Account.

The partnership addresses the issue of publicly visible balances and transactions on Solana, said Tilo Palfner, product lead at Helius. "Public wallets can reveal what someone holds, sends and how they trade," he explained. "Once a wallet is linked to a person, that history can expose their finances to anyone on the internet. That matters for everyday users as much as it does for companies."

Rings are programmable, onchain environments for encrypting money, markets, and finance - without ever leaving Solana

Now live on mainnet. pic.twitter.com/ezCIUCjcbI

The core programs are live on Solana mainnet and have been audited by security firms Certora, OtterSec and ZK Security, according to Helius. The default ring is permissionless, with no global auditor key, and users can recover their private balances directly from Solana without relying on a separate provider to store their encrypted data. Helius is also building Custom Rings, which will let institutions set their own compliance controls and auditor access, with release planned for the fourth quarter of 2026.

#5005. Here’s what happened in crypto today

Need to know what happened in crypto today? Here is the latest news on daily trends and events impacting Bitcoin price, blockchain, DeFi, Web3 and crypto regulation.

Today in crypto, Coinbase and Kalshi have joined four other firms backing the CFTC in a lawsuit over whether US exchanges can list perpetual futures tied to individual stocks, cybersecurity firm CertiK urged exchanges to impose hard dollar limits on hot-wallet holdings following the $6.3 billion Binance hack, and blockchain analytics firm Elliptic reported that North Korean hackers used newly created memecoins to launder stolen funds.

Coinbase, Kalshi and four other companies asked a federal court Monday to accept a brief backing the United States Commodity Futures Trading Commission (CFTC) in a lawsuit over perpetual futures tied to individual US stocks.

According to a proposed amicus brief shared Monday by Coinbase legal representative Molly Abraham, the group also includes Kraken parent Payward, trading companies DRW Holdings and Jump Trading, and crypto investment company Paradigm Operations. The companies argue that stock perpetual futures fall under existing US futures laws.

The filing comes days after a federal judge found that the CFTC likely “exceeded its statutory authority” in allowing single-stock perpetuals to trade as security futures.

The case could determine whether US exchanges can offer retail traders contracts that track individual stocks without expiring.

On Nov. 3, the CFTC announced that single-stock perpetual futures, or stock perps, could begin trading on US-regulated exchanges for the first time.

The Capital Markets Alliance Association (CMAA), a newly formed trade and advocacy group representing traditional financial market participants and whose members include Cboe and Citadel Securities, sued the CFTC last week, challenging the CFTC’s authority to allow the contracts to trade.

Crypto exchanges should impose hard dollar limits on their hot-wallet holdings rather than relying solely on cold-storage ratios to measure security, according to cybersecurity firm CertiK.

The recommendation came as several crypto exchanges recently disclosed how much they held in cold wallets, seeking to reassure customers after the record Binance hack that targeted its hot wallets. Crypto exchange Gate reported that 88.26% of its total assets were in cold storage, while Coinbase said it keeps more than 99% of customer assets offline.

“Cold storage is a useful measure of online exposure, but it is not a standalone security score,” CertiK’s research team told Cointelegraph.

CertiK said crypto exchanges should consider setting hot-wallet caps for each asset and blockchain according to the strictest of three limits: a percentage of customer liabilities, estimated withdrawal needs and an absolute loss ceiling based on the exchange’s financial resources and recovery capabilities.

“Flexibility should support operating liquidity, while hard dollar ceilings and circuit breakers limit potential loss,” they said.

North Korean hackers created memecoins to launder cryptocurrency stolen in the $6.3 billion Binance hack, routing roughly $430,000 through six newly launched tokens on the NEAR blockchain, according to a report from blockchain analytics firm Elliptic.

Between Nov. 5 and 7, the hackers created at least six tokens on NEAR and used liquidity pools on decentralized exchanges to move stolen funds between wallets, Elliptic said. The tokens attracted almost no outside trading and were abandoned shortly after launch.

The hackers used one set of wallets to swap stolen NEAR tokens for the new memecoins, then used another set to sell those memecoins back into the pools for NEAR. The swaps let them move funds without direct wallet-to-wallet transfers, though the transactions remained traceable onchain.

Elliptic said the same laundering technique was also used following the $1.5 billion Bybit hack in 2025, when North Korean-linked actors moved at least $24 million through a newly created token on the TRON blockchain.

Cointelegraph is committed to providing independent, high-quality journalism across the crypto, blockchain, AI, and fintech industries.

All news, reviews, and analyses are produced with full journalistic independence and integrity. For more details on our standards and processes, please read our Editorial Policy.

#5004. Everybody Wants to Do Shabbat Dinner at Erin Foster and Simon Tikhman’s Cozy, Playful LA Home

The recently dropped third season of the hit Netflix rom-com Nobody Wants This begins with a picture of domestic bliss: Kristen Bell and Adam Brody’s characters cheerfully lugging boxes and eating pizza sans dishes—living together at last. The fictional romance between the witty podcaster and “hot rabbi” is inspired by that of series creator Erin Foster and her husband Simon Tikhman, albeit loosely. Like Bell’s Joanne, Foster converted to Judaism before their wedding in 2019; unlike Brody’s Noah, Tikhman is a music executive, not a rabbi—but art is still intermingling with life.

As their onscreen alter egos feather their nest, Foster, Tikhman, and their two-year-old daughter Noa are starring in a new season of their own, moving into a 1920s Los Angeles home that mixes undefinable colors and bold pattern play with the “European flavor” of an old-world estate, per Tikhman. Researching the house’s roots, Foster found Spanish, Mediterranean revival, and English influences, and the couple felt free to embrace all three.

“I don’t tend to like following a set of rules,” Foster says, “whether it be in fashion or in television writing or in design.”

To create more of a grand entrance, the entry foyer was opened up on the second floor and “guests often mistake the entry tile as original,” Pickens Dinwiddy says. The stair runner is by Stark and the vintage mirror is from Nickey Kehoe. The walls are painted in Benjamin Moore’s Soft Chamois.

Tikhman, Foster, and Noa seated on the custom living room sofas upholstered in a Schumacher fabric. “I had girls over for drinks and we sat in the room together,” Foster says. “It’s kind of like the gossip room.”

The sofas flank a custom coffee table in the living room. The vintage chair is from 1stDibs, the ottoman is upholstered in a fabric by Scalamandré, and the vintage floor lamp is from PRB Collection. The sconces are from Aqua Gallery and the curtain fabric is by Rose Tarlow Melrose House. The chandelier is by Cox London. “They handmake each metal leaf,” Pickens Dinwiddy says. The artwork is by Danielle Orchard from Perrotin Gallery.

The multihyphenate cohost of The World’s First Podcast and cofounder of cool-girl fashion line Favorite Daughter acknowledges that she runs in a “circle of really fashion-forward women who have been featured in AD and have elevated taste in art and in design, and it’s very intimidating,” including when it came to selecting a designer. “There starts to be this culture of, ‘Well, you have to use this person,’” Foster says. Instead, she discovered her own on Instagram. Foster cold-messaged Joyce Pickens Dinwiddy, founder of LA’s JDP Interiors (and later the brains behind Favorite Daughter’s Beverly Hills boutique) about masterminding the new-old home’s four-year renovation and redesign.

“They’re a cool couple and they don’t want this stuffy, old-school, fully British-feeling home,” Pickens Dinwiddy says, but Foster and Tikhman still craved classicism their way—something “cozy, young, and playful but sophisticated at the same time.”

“I’ve realized I’m obsessed with the color green,” Foster says. “The color feels a little shocking sometimes to people, but it’s also inviting and it’s also kind of earthy and cozy.” The kitchen is painted in Denali Green from Dunn-Edwards and the cabinets are backed in a Rose Tarlow Melrose House fabric.

Tikhman requested and received a dedicated drinks fridge in the kitchen, filled with clean sodas and seltzers. “Simon was like, ‘I want a Kardashian fridge,’” Foster says.

The kitchen backsplash includes custom hand-painted tiles by Lascaux Tile and the hood is copper. The range is by Lacanche, the fridge is Sub-Zero, and the sink fittings are by Waterstone. The pendants are custom.

The result is a warm, hip house where Pickens Dinwiddy, who’s married to a Brit, introduced charming flourishes familiar to UK country homes. She softened cabinetry in the not-quite olive green kitchen with floral chintz fabric by Rose Tarlow Melrose House and added a skirt beneath the sink in the scullery, a hybrid mud room/potting room traditionally used to water herb pots from the garden. The glass-ceilinged breakfast nook—an “architectural feat,” according to Pickens Dinwiddy, that was lowered into the home via crane and which Foster says “probably added a year to the project”—has been crowned the solarium. With sun drenching its slate blue walls and sculpted medallion details, “it’s a really special place for us to have early dinner with our daughter,” Foster says, and also where she pops open her laptop, morning coffee in hand.

“Erin likes to wake up, have her coffee, pull up her computer, and sit in a room,” Tikhman says. “And her dream was that’s what the solarium was going to be.” The custom banquette is upholstered in a fabric from Perennials Fabrics and topped with pillows covered in Robert Kime fabric. The chairs by Casamidy are upholstered in a Perennials by Rose Tarlow fabric. The pendant is by Vaughan.

“I wanted to do wall-to-wall paneling somewhere,” Pickens Dinwiddy says. “This felt like the place for it so that there wasn’t a wall treatment competing with this beautiful tile floor.” The floor tile is by Ann Sacks. The mudroom bench is upholstered in a fabric by Décors Barbares.

The custom dining room table is surrounded by custom chairs covered in a Jasper fabric. The 1950s chandelier is from Fargo Design and the vintage Swedish sconces are from 1stDibs. The 1970s Danish sideboard is from Chairish and the mirror is custom.

I revel in the regal room names, but the couple is decidedly unfussy about their solarium and scullery—“words that Erin and I never used before building this house,” Tikhman says. “We actually only speak with English accents when we’re at home,” she cracks.

In fact, Foster applauds Tikhman for gently voicing concerns when, in the throes of the project, her old-world sensibilities started to feel a bit too old. Or as Tikhman jokes: “You wanted parking for our horse and carriage.”

“The man cave is my solarium,” Tikhman says. “It was my nonnegotiable, but with Erin’s blessing too.” The sofa is upholstered in Forenza Cinnamon velvet by Romo and the pillows are covered in a fabric by Zak + Fox. The coffee table is vintage.

The bar in the den features custom barstools covered in a Rogers & Goffigon fabric. The table lamp is vintage, the sink fittings are by Waterworks, and the walls are painted in Galway Bay by Dunn-Edwards.

“Simon was like, ‘Listen, I love you. I trust you. I know that you’re going to make this house beautiful, but I just keep thinking that I’m going to be walking around in basketball shorts and sneakers and a backwards hat and you’re in your pajamas all day long. Are we going to be in a castle that doesn’t feel like our personalities?’” Foster recalls. “It really woke me up to this idea that you have to balance everything.”

Out went an old-fashioned pull-chain toilet in the powder room; in came modern Swedish and Italian light fixtures, plus Tikhman’s passion project: making theirs a stealth smart home.

“The bedroom is very important because we’re people who like to be in the bedroom,” Tikhman says. He issues a clarification: “We like to watch TV in bed.” The bed is upholstered in a Rose Uniacke fabric and flanked with custom sconces. The shade fabric is by Jasper and the sofa is upholstered in a Claremont Furnishing fabric. The vintage pendant is from 1stDibs and the walls are painted in Benjamin Moore’s Gray Wisp.

In the primary bathroom, the hand-painted wall mural is by James Mobley. The vanity pendants are custom.

The vintage marble cabochon tiles are from Neolithic Materials. The tub is by The Water Monopoly.

Behind peony-printed Papier wallpaper and vintage sconces from 1stDibs, “the brains of the house are so advanced,” says Tikhman, cofounder and CEO of The Core Entertainment, a music company that includes artist management, publishing, a record label, and brand partnerships. He’s giddy over no-show speakers baked into the walls, a dining room mirror that slides open to reveal a TV viewable from the kitchen—so he can simultaneously watch and chat with Foster as she cooks her go-to tofu with broccoli (“Soy is no longer being vilified,” she says)—and an AI assistant named Nicola who can modulate temperature, lights, and of course music.

In the primary suite sitting room, the chair is upholstered in a Schumacher velvet and the walls are painted in Benjamin Moore’s Mustang.

Tikhman’s shoe collection in the primary closet

Alongside the revamp of their family home, the couple collaborated on season three of Nobody Wants This, with Foster writing, executive-producing, and making a cameo as an unhinged podcast fan (her impersonations in the writer’s room demanded to be seen), and Tikhman executive-producing the season-three soundtrack, curating unreleased tracks from the likes of Miley Cyrus and Ellie Goulding for the rom-com’s sweeping, big-love moments. Tikhman is used to managing artists, but jokes that producing the show’s soundtrack for the second time was tougher. “I want to make sure that my wife and her team are happy, and managing Erin is way more difficult,” he grins. “She’s my artist.”

“I’m trying to dress [Noa] into a cool tomboy, but she seems to really love putting on lots of jewelry and carrying a handbag,” Foster says. “I think we’ll adjust the room based on who she becomes.” In the nursery, the daybed is upholstered in a fabric by Namay Samay. The walls are covered in Lover’s Bloom by Papier and the pendant is by Beauvamp.

In Noa’s bathroom, the Hector Finch sconces feature hand-painted shades by artist Sarah Blomfield. The sink fittings are by Waterworks, the pendant is vintage, and the floor tiles are by Tabarka.

In a recent episode of Nobody Wants This, a pool party at Joanne and Noah’s spins out of control. The couple balks at first, but “I kinda like being the house that everybody comes to,” Joanne decides. Foster and Tikhman emphatically agree. “We do not want an untouchable home,” Foster says. “I don’t want to be a coaster house. I’m okay with a ring on the table.”

For a recent San Francisco 49ers game, Tikhman invited friends and Noa to “soft-launch” his “man cave,” a freestanding structure lined in Forenza Cinnamon velvet sofas by Roma meant to evoke the “Ralph Lauren vibes” of a high-end bar.

Foster says sharing a home with Tikhman “unlocked my inner trad-wife hostess.”

“I’m on a group chat called Shabbat Moms,” she says. Of its 12 members, “we’ve probably hosted more than anybody. We’ll have 30 people over, and there’s kids running everywhere, and it’s the best kind of chaos ever.”

Foster, Tikhman, and Noa in the garden designed by Emily Cooper with Balanced Ground Design.

#5003. Tesla Semi catches fire on I-880 with no crash, closes freeway for 10 hours

A Tesla Semi caught fire on Interstate 880 in Hayward, California, on Tuesday evening, and there was no crash. The electric truck’s lithium-ion battery burned long enough to keep the southbound freeway closed for about 10 hours.

The driver got out unhurt. But a Class 8 electric truck catching fire on its own, in traffic, is something Tesla and its growing list of Semi customers need to explain.

The fire broke out at around 5:35 p.m. PT in the southbound lanes near Route 92. CHP Hayward then closed I-880 in both directions, citing “the potential risk of hazardous chemical smoke,” and posted these photos from the scene:

🚨 TRAFFIC ALERT – I-880 FULL CLOSURE 🚨A Tesla semi-truck has caught fire on I-880, resulting in a complete shutdown of both northbound and southbound lanes due to the potential risk of hazardous chemical smoke. pic.twitter.com/O01z32nxpZ

The driver told officers the truck caught fire and that they didn’t know why. No other vehicles were involved.

Northbound reopened Tuesday night. Southbound took much longer. CHP initially warned that the battery could burn for 15 to 18 hours, but crews beat that estimate, and CHP Hayward posted that all lanes were open at around 3:30 a.m. Wednesday.

The CHP photos show firefighters laying foam around the burned-out tractor, with the front of a Premier Trailer Leasing trailer charred behind it. The truck’s operator hasn’t been identified, and Tesla, which doesn’t have a press office, hasn’t commented. The cause is under investigation.

We’ve been here before. In August 2024, a Tesla-operated Semi caught fire on I-80 near Emigrant Gap while driving from Livermore to Tesla’s facility in Sparks, Nevada. At the time, no crash was reported. The NTSB later found the truck had left the road on a curve, hit a delineator post and a tree, and its battery ignited after the impact. Firefighters used about 50,000 gallons of water, and the lanes stayed closed for up to 15 hours.

Hayward is different. Without a crash, the focus shifts to the truck itself: the battery pack, the high-voltage system, the drive units, or something as ordinary as a tire or brake fire that spread.

And the timing isn’t great for Tesla. The company only started volume production of the Semi in Nevada this year, after nearly a decade of delays. WattEV ordered 370 Semis in May, mostly for drayage at the Port of Oakland, just a few miles up I-880 from where Tuesday’s truck burned. In September, Tesla was also named lead supplier on a 2,500-truck ZET SCALE order.

First, some perspective, because this fire is going to get used as an anti-EV talking point. Truck fires are common. According to NFPA data, US fire departments responded to an average of 15,462 large-truck fires per year between 2018 and 2022. That’s about 42 a day, in a fleet that’s overwhelmingly diesel. Mechanical failure was a factor in 44% of them, and only about 2% were tied to a collision.

The same goes for cars. NFPA and DOT data put the US average at one vehicle fire for every 17 million miles traveled, and combustion vehicles make up the vast majority of that fleet. Tesla claims one fire per 140 million miles for its own vehicles. That’s Tesla’s number, but independent data, like government figures we’ve covered before, points the same way: gas vehicles catch fire more often than EVs.

This is not the main reason that pretty much all the other trucks have moved to LFP, but it is still one more reason that looks like a good idea.

Also, designing your own cells is higher risk... at this point, can they even switch supplier?

But that doesn’t get the Semi off the hook. Diesel fires happen across millions of trucks. Tesla doesn’t disclose Semi deliveries, but PepsiCo, its launch customer, was up to about 86 trucks in 2024, and volume production only started a few months ago. With a fleet that small, one fire with no crash is a big data point. It’s also the second Semi fire to shut down a California interstate in about two years.

So we need the cause. If a tire, a brake, or the trailer caught fire and it spread to the tractor, that’s a regular trucking problem. If the pack went into thermal runaway on its own, Tesla needs to find out why before tens of thousands of these trucks hit the road, and fire departments need a better plan than letting a loaded truck burn on a freeway for half the night.

The NTSB investigated the last one. It should look at this one too, and Tesla should publish what it finds. Will it?

Electric trucks and cars are only as clean as the electricity charging them. If you’re driving an EV, powering it with home solar can cut your fuel costs to nearly zero. With electricity rates up almost 10% last year and expected to keep climbing, going solar is one of the best ways to protect yourself against rising costs. #5002. Poland’s lower house backs sanctions bill covering crypto freezes

The bill is now before the Senate and would broaden the grounds for adding people and companies to Poland’s national sanctions list.

Poland’s lower house passed a bill that would broaden the country’s sanctions framework and explicitly include crypto assets among the funds authorities can freeze when imposing sanctions.

The Sejm approved the government-backed Act on Restrictive Measures on Friday by 240 votes to 202, with one abstention, and forwarded it to the Senate on Monday, according to official parliamentary records. The bill explicitly includes crypto assets, as defined under the European Union’s Markets in Crypto-Assets Regulation (MiCA), among financial funds that authorities could freeze when imposing sanctions.

Poland’s interior minister would oversee the national sanctions list, with sanctions-listing decisions taking effect the day after authorities publish a designation. Affected individuals and companies could challenge the decision in administrative court, but the bill allows enforcement before a court ruling.

Poland already maintains a national sanctions list under a 2022 law aimed at countering support for Russia’s invasion of Ukraine. The bill would expand the grounds for imposing national sanctions to include certain foreign interference and information manipulation that pose a serious threat to national security.

The Senate has 30 days to approve, amend or reject the legislation. If it takes no action, Poland’s Constitution treats the bill as approved in the form passed by the Sejm, allowing it to proceed to the president for consideration.

#5001. This Next-Generation Retinol Has All the Same Benefits But is Gentler on Skin

Here’s all you need to know before adding to cart.

Women's Health may earn commission from the links on this page, but we only feature products we believe in. Why Trust Us?

There are only a handful of skincare ingredients proven to work—and retinol is one of them. It’s part of a larger category of ingredients called retinoids, all derived from vitamin A and considered the gold standard for transforming skin’s texture, clarity, tone, and overall appearance.

There’s a major caveat, though: Because it can be irritating, retinol can’t be used by everyone and you have to be very careful when combining it with other ingredients like niacinamide, vitamin C, and certain exfoliating acids. Whether you have sensitive, acne-prone, or even normal skin, holy-grail retinol can be decidedly less holy if it leaves your complexion red, dry, peeling, or flaky (collectively known as the dreaded ‘retinol purge’).

It’s not just retinol, BTW. All forms of vitamin A, even low-strength versions, have the potential to cause some degree of irritation, especially for newbies taking the plunge (most derms recommend beginning with a lower concentration of the ingredient a few times a week). That’s because retinoids essentially ask skin cells to behave differently by influencing cell turnover, differentiation, and collagen production to improve texture, fine lines, and photoaging, explains Santa Monica-, and Beverly Hills-based board-certified dermatologist Ava Shamban, MD. “In the beginning when using them, there’s an adjustment period, called retinization, that temporarily disrupts the skin barrier, increasing dryness, redness, flaking, and sensitivity, or, as some people call it, the retinoid uglies.” As skin adjusts to the ingredient, these side effects usually taper off and irritation improves.

Meet the experts: Ava Shamban, MD, is a board-certified dermatologist based in Santa Monica and Beverly Hills. Melissa Fox is a celebrity master esthetician and Founder of Flawless by Melissa Fox in Miami and West Palm Beach. Debra Luftman, MD, is a board-certified dermatologist at Schweiger Dermatology in Beverly Hills.

Still, some skin types, especially those with compromised skin barriers or dry, sensitive, eczema- or rosacea-prone skin simply can’t tolerate vitamin A-derived ingredients, no matter how low and slow they go. For that reason, these ingredients have earned an unfair reputation as overly irritating, claims Melissa Fox, a celebrity master esthetician and Founder of Flawless by Melissa Fox in Miami and West Palm Beach. “But you can have a powerful vitamin A treatment without feeling like your skin has to suffer for results.”

That’s because there’s a little-known gentler version of retinol that’s just starting to pop up in over-the-counter products. Known as hydroxypinacolone retinoate (HPR), this next-generation retinoic acid ester is a virtually less-irritating molecule and just may be the secret for those that have been forced to swear off retinol.

Intrigued, I spoke with two dermatologists and an aesthetician, all well-versed in the world of retinoids, about what makes HPR different yet beneficial, and everything you need to know before making it your new favorite skincare ingredient.

So what is this magical molecule? HPR, also listed as ‘granactive retinoid’ on some ingredient labels, is the newest member of the retinoid family and is quickly becoming a go-to alternative to stronger, harsher iterations. “It’s a newer retinoic acid ester that may provide some of the anti-aging benefits of traditional retinoids just with less irritation,” says Debra Luftman, MD, a board-certified dermatologist at Schweiger Dermatology in Beverly Hills.

For traditional retinol to work its magic, it must first convert into retinaldehyde and then into retinoic acid, Dr. Shamban explains. For example, tretinoin, available only by prescription, is already retinoic acid and therefore considered your strongest option. Similarly, HPR comes straight out of the bottle as an already accessible version that doesn’t require any conversions. “This multi-step conversion process is typically what causes the significant discomfort and reaction process,” she adds. Essentially, fewer conversion steps equals less irritation. (But Dr. Shamban is quick to note that irritation also depends on the molecule's biological activity, concentration, delivery system, skin barrier condition, and level of sensitivity.)

Fox adds that HPR is particularly promising for anyone who want the benefits of vitamin A, such as smoother texture, improved appearance of fine lines, and more even-looking skin, but has struggled with traditional formulas. “Early research suggests it may offer good tolerability, which is exciting, but the clinical evidence is still developing, so I wouldn’t call it completely irritation-free.” One study shows that HPR demonstrates well-tolerated anti-aging effects as a gentler option, especially for sensitive skin.

What remains to be seen with any decisive clarity is however HPR compares results-wise with tried-and-true versions of vitamin A like retinol or commonly-prescribed adapalene or tretinoin. While it shows promising activity, Dr. Luftman says there isn't enough clinical evidence to rank it against retinol, retinaldehyde, or prescription tretinoin, which remains most studied.

So why would a skincare brand choose HPR over retinol, with decades of evidence supporting its benefits? From a formulation standpoint, there are several reasons, suggests Dr. Shamban. HPR is oil-soluble and can be incorporated into cosmetically-elegant delivery systems, including emollient-based serums. “The finished product is what actually goes on the skin,” she says, so the nicer to use, the more likely you are to use it. On the other hand, formulating with traditional retinol can be challenging. “It’s sensitive to light, oxygen, and other destabilizing conditions, so HPR may offer those practical formulation advantages.”

Working HPR into your skincare routine isn’t hard, and it follows many of the same rules as traditional retinoids. When considering adding it to your regimen, start gradually. Dr. Luftman recommends applying a small amount to clean, dry skin about three nights per week. “Slowly increase the frequency as your skin adjusts—consistency matters more than concentration.”

Moisturizer is also a must. “You can try the popular sandwich method, which involves applying your cream before and after HPR, to help improve comfort,” suggests Dr. Shaman, “or just apply it after.” She recommends looking for a moisturizer that contains barrier-supporting ingredients, such as ceramides, glycerin, or hyaluronic acid. “You’ll also want to avoid using strong exfoliating acids on the same evening during the introductory phase.” However, it’s perfectly safe to use HPR alongside niacinamide, peptides, or retinol alternatives like bakuchiol.

Like retinol, always use HPR with a daily broad-spectrum sunscreen that has an SPF of at least 30 in the morning, since the ingredient can make the skin more sensitive to the sun.

Elizabeth Arden Retinol + HPR Ceramide Capsules Rapid Skin Renewing Serum

Who doesn’t love a single-use ampoule? And when it contains HPR, retinol, and ceramides, all the better. This gentle formula goes hard to work to reduce the visible appearance of wrinkles in seven days, without the irritation or dryness that retinol can leave behind. Instead, it improves the skin’s texture and tone without any redness or flaking.

The Ordinary Granactive Retinoid 2% Emulsion

A more affordable option worth considering is this serum, which hits on the common signs of aging and uneven tone and texture. Dr. Luftman particularly likes that the product contains HPR in a moisturizing squalane base, so it’s appealing for those with dry or sensitive skin who have difficulty tolerating conventional retinol.

Dry skin and retinol can, unfortunately, go hand in hand. While skincare experts often suggest applying a moisturizer after retinol, why not make life easy and ditch the extra step? This lightweight oil is powered by HPR, four hyaluronic acid derivatives, peptides, and skin-friendly oils to support the skin barrier and deliver a dose of moisture where its needed most. We also like how the oil creates a natural glow and a more refined complexion by morning that improves with each use.

HPR isn’t exclusive to just the face. In fact, it’s making its way into body lotions and serums for the same reason it’s becoming the ingredient du jour in skin care. This potent-yet-gentle lightweight retinoid serum repairs the skin and leaves it better hydrated, silky soft, and smooth.

The delicate skin around the eyes is often also home to fine lines, wrinkles, thinning skin, and discoloration, which is why an eye cream with HPR is a must. Formulated specifically for the eye area, this sensitive skin-approved cream also counts peptides and plant extracts among its hero ingredients to reverse the appearance of tired, aging eyes.

There’s a lot of skin-boosting potential with HPR, but before you slather it all over, read the ingredient label, since some products also contain retinol or retinoids. While HPR can absolutely serve as the sole retinoid in your skincare routine, or be paired with other vitamin A derivatives, Dr. Luftman suggests anyone with sensitive skin use it alone to minimize potential irritation. “There’s sufficient evidence that combining HPR with a retinoid does produce better results,” she adds.

And bear in mind that just like a retinoid, HPR takes weeks for the skin to adjust and for results to show, so patience is a virtue. Your skin may be dry initially, but Dr. Shamban says persistent burning, significant redness, or peeling is a sign to stop and reassess, not push through.

Bottom line: If you’ve tried what seems like every retinol and retinoid out there and still haven’t found the perfect one that doesn’t leave your skin dry, red, and irritated, then it may be time to give HPR a try. Don’t expect an overnight miracle, but you may notice significantly less irritation, making it easier to tolerate. Ultimately, Dr. Shamban says the most effective retinoid is the one that delivers results—and that your skin can tolerate.

In short, the product you’ll actually use.

#5000. Is Your Penis Getting Smaller as You Age? This Common Factor Could Hide One to Two Inches.

IT PROBABLY SOUNDS like a nightmare scenario to think your penis is getting shorter. Maybe not even being visible at all. But it can happen as you age, and when it does, doctors refer to it as a buried penis.

Now it's not that your penis is physically getting shorter. It's just that most of it is hidden. More on that below.

CONVINCED YOU LOST an inch? Thankfully, your penis isn’t disappearing; it’s just hidden beneath fat tissue.

Justin Dubin, MD, urologist and men’s health specialist at Baptist Health, says that generally when you can’t see the penis, it’s usually because of low abdominal tissue surrounding the penis.

Essentially, your penis is still its regular size, but it doesn’t extend outside the body. Drogo Montague, MD, a urologist in Ohio tells MH what that looks like. “There is an opening where the penis would normally hang, and the head is usually flush with skin, but there’s no shaft outside of the body.”

Now you don’t just wake up one day with inches of your penis vanished. The effect occurs on a continuum, says Dr. Montague. You might notice at first, for instance, that your penis seems to get shorter and shorter. (Though, this is not the same as the small amount of shrinkage that some men see with age, which is due to skin cells around the penis becoming less elastic.)

“This is a serious condition because it will inhibit urination, intimacy, and personal hygiene,” says Scott Miller, MD, urologist and medical director of Wellstar Urology. “It can also strongly impact self-esteem and overall emotional health.”

MOST CASES OF buried penis are due to one thing: obesity.

While the rates of buried penises haven’t been thoroughly studied, Dr. Montague sees more men in his office today dealing with the issue than he has in the past. His booked-up calendar is no coincidence. The World Health Organization reported that 1 in 7 people is living with obesity. And even though we are living in the golden age of GLP-1s, half of adults worldwide are expected to be overweight or obese by 2050.

The penis itself has two erection chambers. About one-third is inside the body and two-thirds are outside in the penile shaft. When you’re overweight or with severe obesity—and noticing a change in length—what’s changing is basically how much of your penis is inside your body and how much is outside.

“With extreme obesity, it’s like the obese body engulfs the penis. A full buried penis is when the entire penis shaft is buried below the surface of the skin,” says Dr. Montague. So it's still there, but you just can't see it all.

There are a few factors, some starting at birth.

There is a low chance of a baby being born with abnormalities in which the ligaments that attach to the penis are weak. Fortunately, Dr. Dubin says this is usually identified and surgically corrected early on.

Another cause can be “any surgery on your penis” that’s gone wrong, says Dr. Dubin. That includes circumcision, a procedure done on 71.2 percent of American guys are circumcised at birth (or even as an adult). If a doctor does not remove an appropriate amount of foreskin, there is a risk of the penis dropping back into the fatty area surrounding it.

But any procedure that involves the penis runs the risk of scar tissue and possible infection. That includes penile injections and penis enlargement.

“The ironic part is that while a lot of these things try to make your penis look bigger or be bigger, the complication is it can make your penis actually smaller and cause buried penis,” says Dr. Dubin.

Lymphedema can cause swelling in the scrotum. As lymph fluid accumulates, you might see the makings of a buried penis.

Also known as acne inversa, the skin condition can create small and extremely painful lumps under the skin. Lumps usually appear in the armpits, inner thighs, and—you guessed it—your groin. This happens because the body mistakenly attacks hair follicles in the sweat glands, causing pockets of pus and other scarring.

Sometimes when the prostate is removed due to prostate cancer surgery, men may lose about an inch of penile length. “That’s the result of pulling the urethra up to attach to the bladder,” says Dr. Montague. Still, it seems like most penile shortening rebounds as time passes after your surgery, so don't let that stop you from getting your prostate checked.

The condition occurs when scar tissue forms in the penis and it causes a curved erection. The curvature may give the appearance of a shorter length. The issue often arises because of injury to the penis during sex or micro-tears that accumulated from sports or physical accidents affecting the genital area. Fortunately, if diagnosed early, there are drugs and procedures that could straighten out the penis. Read more about it here.

WHEN YOU HAVE a buried penis, certain activities become more difficult. Right away, you might find it hard to pee. Dr. Dubin says guys may not be able to get the stream started and might have poor flow. Buried penis can also cause trouble in the form of dribbling and incontinence.

The area is also tough to clean, which leads to a host of issues that might trigger fungal or bacterial infections or skin breakdown. Buried penis is associated, for example, with a rare skin condition called lichen sclerosus. It’s an inflammatory disorder that causes itchy patches. The skin that hosts these patches becomes very delicate.

Beyond that, penetrative sex remains an ongoing issue. “These men can’t have sex or sex is very difficult,” says Dr. Montague.

Mentally, having a buried penis can cause depression and self-esteem issues. “Most men associate their penis with their masculinity, and they associate fertility with masculinity,” says Dr. Dubin. “These kinds of issues can have strong mental health implications.”

IF YOUR BURIED penis is obesity-related, losing weight will definitely help. But keep in mind that, even after weight loss, you might have excess skin and tissue that keeps your penis buried.

“Sometimes, unfortunately, in very obese people, the damage has already been done, and more aggressive treatment options may be required,” Dr. Dubin. In that scenario, you may need surgery—which is also an option for people who don’t lose weight. Dr. Miller says surgeons would focus on removing the surrounding fat and tissue and repositioning the penis.

“That allows the penis to come outside of the body,” says Dr. Montague. “In most buried penis surgeries, skin and fat are cut out.”

Having a buried penis can be embarrassing to talk about, Dr. Dubin says. It's disturbing to have this happen and it can take a toll on your well-being. If you feel it's getting in the way of living fully, consider talking with a counselor or other mental health professional. It's also crucial to see a doctor if you’re having urinary, hygiene, or sexual health issues because of a buried penis.

She also writes the best-selling Substack newsletter Two Truths and is a co-founder of the non-profit Chamber of Mothers.
