#4999. 15 Chic and Surprising Christmas Tree Decorating Ideas

Nothing says the holidays have arrived like a glowing pine strung with tinsel. If you’re looking for fresh inspiration, this collection of designer-approved Christmas tree decorating ideas has it all. From playful color palettes to chic lighting ideas and nostalgic personalization, designers are sharing their best tips for decorating full-sized, pencil, and even tabletop trees. Like a freshly pruned fir, their ideas are lush and abundant: oversized bows, classic red ornaments, blue Christmas trees, and velvet ribbon turned into handmade ornaments are just a few of their favorite festive touches. Whether you prefer a real tree, artificial Christmas tree, or even a flocked Christmas tree, lean minimalist or fully embrace maximalist holiday decor, we’ve rounded up our favorite Christmas decorating ideas to help you deck your halls in style.

To personalize your tree, take inspiration from Chanel Sansregret of the Charleston-based Studio Miles Design who purchases frame ornaments with the year to fill with photos of her children every holiday season. “For me, there’s something especially moving about hanging those memories on the branches and seeing so many stages of their childhood together in one place,” she shares. “As a designer, I love a beautifully considered tree, but the sentimental details are what give it soul,” Sansregret states.

“There’s something so special about turning a corner and finding another beautifully decorated tree,” says Emily Hewett of A Well Dressed Home based in Dallas and Kansas City. In her son’s bedroom, she opted for a slender pencil tree given the room’s space constraints. “My son’s tree sits directly in front of his bedroom window, which faces the street; one of my favorite details is seeing it all lit up from the front of our home at night,” Hewett says. A pencil tree has a small footprint, yet Hewett notes it brings a little extra Christmas magic to both the inside and outside of the home.

To make a Christmas tree feel like an integral part of a room, take design cues from the room itself. Here, Hewett adorned her tree with a collection of glass bauble Christmas ornaments that she’s curated over the years. “The color palette features varying shades of blue, green, and pink, along with clear iridescent ornaments that almost resemble delicate bath bubbles,” she says. The Christmas theme was inspired by her living room palette. “Every color was intentionally pulled from the wallpapers and fabrics in my living room, creating a Christmas tree that feels like a natural extension of the space,” Hewett says.

When decorating your Christmas tree in a room with dark walls, Renee DiSanto of the Chicago area–based Park & Oak recommends using lots of twinkle lights and weaving them into the tree in a zigzag pattern. “I tend to opt for a warm white light rather than a cool blue tone for a more cozy, inviting look,” she shares. Here, the designer used bright colored decorations, a bead garland, a simple tree skirt—and plenty of twinkle lights. “A well-lit tree will always pop against dark walls,” DiSanto says.

“Bows are such a classic, but including them on a tree in a dramatic scale is what makes something traditional feel unexpected and even a bit whimsical,” says Amy Studebaker of Amy Studebaker Design. She approaches Christmas tree decorating the same way she approaches designing a home: ”With a fresh take on traditional, layers and layers of charm, and a little sprinkle of the unexpected.” For a fun and festive look, replace a traditional tree topper with a large bow and adorn branches with smaller, coordinating bows.

Brown has become the neutral this season, adding cozy sophistication to interiors and now, to holiday decor. Anastasia Casey of The Interior Collective loves the color for its warmth and versatility, and used it as the primary color on this tree. “I layered matte and metallic brown ornaments with neutrals—think muddy hues of bone, chocolate, and champagne—to create depth and avoid a flat look,” Casey shares. While the metallics catch light, the browns ground the palette, resulting in a tree that Casey says is both elevated and inviting.

To add charm and a touch of whimsy to a Christmas tree, Anastasia Casey of The Interior Collective looks for ornaments that feel collected and tell a story, whether they’re vintage finds, handmade pieces, or unique shapes. “In order to make it feel cohesive, I recommend mixing textures and scales, like pairing glass baubles with fabric or wood. It brings a sense of personality and keeps the tree from feeling too uniform,” she shares. Casey also loves incorporating dynamic elements like ribbon or bells to add movement and playfulness.

What is the correct order to decorate a Christmas tree?

The first thing in Griffin’s step-by-step approach is adding twinkle lights to the tree, then tucking in larger ornaments. “Next, I add the smaller decorative ornaments, and finally, I add the tinsel or bows on the edges of the tree limbs,” Griffin shares. Studebaker says you should have fun with decorating and not take the process too seriously; “It can still be sophisticated, but it should absolutely be a happy and playful process along the way,” the designer says.

What are the most popular Christmas tree decorations?

Designers say German glass ornaments, Christopher Radko ornaments, cascading ribbon, festive bows, and warm white lights are among the most popular Christmas tree decorations. Metallic decorations, whether gold, silver, or copper, are another favorite. “I love decorating with metallic ornaments because they reflect the light so beautifully,” Bonney-James shares.

For Bonney-James, a timeless Christmas theme includes a fresh tree. “I’ll trade messy needles for an aromatic, nostalgic experience any day,” she says. Another must-have are warm white lights, which is why she recommends checking the temperature of LED lights to make sure they don’t appear blue. The addition of silver, gold, and white decorations completes the timeless look and adds festive shine.

#4997. Alan Cumming Can’t Be Tamed

At 61, the Traitors host has made a career of playing by his own rules. From leading his dark new series Tip Toe to reprising his X-Men role as Nightcrawler in Avengers: Doomsday, he's always eager to take on a different form.

Our product picks are editor-tested, expert-approved. We may earn a commission through links on our site. Why Trust Us?

“I’M GEARING UP towards hacking it all off,” Alan Cumming says as he twists a finger around an errant silver tendril of hair dangling across his forehead. “I usually like to end a job and do something—get it cut off or get it colored. Something.”

The 61-year-old Tony-winning-Broadway-star-turned-Emmy-winning-reality-TV-host is the consummate chameleon. In just the past year, he’s played the “dandy Scottish laird” host of The Traitors: New Blood, reprised his role as the X-Men’s resident lizard-like teleporting mutant Nightcrawler in Avengers: Doomsday, and took on one of his most emotionally and physically challenging roles as gay bar owner Leo in Tip Toe.

The impulse to change—both on screen and off—is therapeutic. “Over the years, I've had so many different hairstyles, I think, as a reaction to my childhood trauma about my father's obsession with my hair,” he says. Cumming’s memoir, Not My Father’s Son, opens with a scene of his father taking a rusty pair of sheep shears to his temple in a fit of rage. “They were blunt and dirty and they cut my skin, but my father shaved my head with them, holding me down like an animal,” Cumming writes in the first chapter.

The trauma followed him into early adulthood, and he experienced visceral anxiety whenever he got his hair cut. Eventually, however, the act of transformation transformed Cumming. “Hair was always something I felt I had no control over as a child,” he says. “So I wanted to have as much control over it and change it as much as possible, almost in a slightly perverse way.”

Of course, the choice to change isn’t always his, and sometimes the choice is taken away from him. It was “painful,” he says, to be unable to change his hair when he was working on certain projects, like his seven seasons on The Good Wife. “Over the years, I've had so many different hairstyles, but I don't often have my own hair,” Cumming adds. “It's usually for some job.”

Today, however, his natural silver hair seems purposefully unstyled as his fingers coax through it. That is, when he isn’t using his hands to assume the statuesque pose of his Traitors persona or demonstrate the boxing stance from his Marvel superhero training. Simply talking about a performance becomes a performance, his eyes bulging behind a pair of thick-rimmed black glasses and his arms gesturing widely underneath his faded red T-shirt as he describes the immense physicality required of these recent roles.

The walls of his Scottish home are white and surprisingly sparse. You’d have no idea that the person living there is now, by his own admission, best known for “swanning around” a Scottish castle “like an insane little prince” in increasingly outrageous outfits (so outrageous, in fact, that Traitors contestants often applaud when he enters the room after a wardrobe change). Or perhaps the walls are white so he stands out all the more. Either way, the Scottish charm is working overtime on a Sunday evening.

“I think both with my hair and, more lately with what I wear, I do sort of feel like a little dolly that people dress up,” he says before adding, with a good-natured laugh, “I don't mind it.”

In a brief moment between transformations, Cumming sat down with Men’s Health to discuss his ever-changing style on Traitors, how he trained for Avengers: Doomsday, and what he considers to be the ultimate fountain of youth.

MEN’S HEALTH: You often talk about your fashion choices with each Traitors season, but what goes into choosing your hairstyle?

ALAN CUMMING: There was a feeling that because we were doing the civilian version, we could reboot and bring things down a little. So, we decided just to go a little more simple and classic with the hair. But it was hilarious, because every morning I would walk downstairs and say, ‘This is toning it down. What the fuck?’ I guess I wasn't dressed as a coffin or a bride. The plan was to go back to the dandy Scottish laird a little, but it still was pretty mental. But that's why I didn't have lots of color and things in my hair. But we're about to start another Traitors season in a couple of weeks, so all bets are off again.

MH: You're in your 60s now, and you still have wonderful hair. Did you ever have a fear of going bald when you were younger, or would that have maybe been freeing in a way?

AC: I've never feared going bald. I don't think I would care. It's easy when you’re not the bald person saying this, but I don't quite think going bald has the same power it used to. I feel like so many people shave their heads nowadays anyway. And there are loads of actors I know who are bald and wear wigs all the time. When you're an actor, it's obviously easier—and actually probably better—to be bald, because you get more hairdo options.

MH: Outside of Traitors, you're also reprising your role as Nightcrawler in Avengers: Doomsday. How was doing your own stunts in your 60s?

AC: I did some stunt work, but I didn't do all my own stunts. Please don't think that. There are lots of things I'm not allowed to tell you about. I got into trouble for giving the game away earlier on. But I mean, I fight some people. There's loads of fighting in it. If you enjoy fighting and you enjoy superheroes, this movie's for you.

I loved the training. They always train you for much more than you actually need, but I did it. I was boxing with these French boys. And I’d just made a film in France, so they were very impressed with my French and the fact that I could box a little and not have a heart attack.

It’s really funny when people, especially younger people, are so impressed by your fitness. I'm quite bendy as well, so people are always impressed by my flexibility. And I guess I am 61, but, I mean, I'm not dead. I'm not a thousand years old. I've luckily not had any sort of physical things going wrong. I don't really feel any different, to be honest, but it’s also hilarious when young people are just amazed that you've been able to stand up and not die.

MH: How long were you doing this boxing training for?

AC: I only did a week or so with those boys. But I used to take boxing lessons in New York, and I really want to get back into it again. It's such good exercise, especially before you actually get up and spar—all the core stuff and strength stuff—and it's such fun as well.

My boxing teacher was called Mo, and he'd been a big sort of world champion. But one day I went to the gym, and he was really pissed off because he’d seen a music video with some rappers and singers, and they were all playing boxers. He said, "They were just terrible."

And he went, "Drake. Drake was the worst."

And he said to me, "You could take Drake." So now, every time I see Drake, I just think, “I could take you.” Drake better watch out.

MH: Let him know it's on sight. How else did you prepare your body for Avengers?

AC: I actually stay pretty fit all the time. When I'm doing The Traitors, I go to the gym every day, or I do three days gym, two days swim or yoga. There have been times in my life when I've had to get really, really super fit or super skinny or something, so you go nuts. But I like being fit, and I like stretching. I like doing things like that, so I’m pretty constant.

Filming Tip Toe was much more physically demanding, because I knew I was going to have a lot of emotional weight to carry. It was a really disturbing show. So, I would exercise a lot, but I would also get two Thai massages on the weekend because my body was so tense.

If you do a film like X-Men and you're jumping around, yes, sometimes your body can get hurt in that way, but emotionally it wasn't really very demanding, whereas Tip Toe was so intense. Physically, I'm so stressed a lot of the time, and David Morrissey, the other actor, is shoving me around. So, my body was all screwed up. What's really important in those situations is to plan ahead and to make space for you to give your body some chance to recover.

MH: How do you think about your body differently as you take on each new role?

AC: I'm a very physical actor. I think about how the person physically behaves and how they move and stand and… [laughs]. I'm laughing because my husband is just over there, and he always watches me in things and says, "Oh, you look so different. You just disappeared into this character." And then there'll be a scene when I'm running, and he can tell it's me because I run in a weird way. My running always gives me away. But you know who's got the weirdest run ever? Woody Allen. I was once in a film with him and he had to run, and I've never seen anything like it. It just was like, "How is this all happening? How are all these limbs managing to propel someone forward?" It was the craziest thing.

But I'm always very conscious of the physicality of a character. The Traitors is a big example. He's very erect, very sort of grand, quite dancy. He moves like a dancer. And then in Tip Toe, it was really fascinating becoming more cowed as the show went on. He got less confident, so I got more crushed.

With Nightcrawler, he's really fascinating because he's like a little weird lizard or something. So it's a lot of neck stuff. In the first film, I did a lot of work with a movement person to develop the language of that. So, I just remembered all that stuff for Avengers. I suppose it’s muscle memory. After 22 years, I really did remember all that stuff. Also, they gave me a gray wig, which I thought was hilarious. That was a concession to my elder statesman-like thing. I've got gray hair as Nightcrawler now, which is just hilarious. Nothing else has changed, just his hair has grown gray.

MH: You mentioned your longtime husband, Grant. What do you think makes a relationship last?

AC: Having a laugh, being open and kind. Being kind is the biggest thing, but being honest and always trying to talk things through and having a laugh. Also, having time apart.

When I go away to make a film or whatever, we don't see each other for a few weeks. It's actually really great because you miss that person, and you look forward to seeing them and you value the time you do have. It's not just about missing them, but you remember the things that you've always liked, and you're constantly reassessing your relationship because you're looking forward to seeing that person again. Also, it means you maintain your own identity. Obviously, we're a very strong couple, but we have our own lives as well.

So it's a combination of those things, but I really do think you can't underestimate how much being able to laugh is. Also, I think it's good to still have sex. Sex and laughter. I think if either of those go or both of those go, get out, taxi for one. Because they're both the same thing, I suppose—they're sensual pleasures. Those are very important for me, anyway.

MH: Agreed. And so, you mentioned that you're 61 now—

MH: As we all should. But tell me, what's the best part about being 61?

AC: There are lots of nice things. I mean, partly that I forget I'm 61. On the show I'm doing right now, I feel like I can just chat with the kids on the crew who are in their 20s. For me, what's great is being in a position where I'm able to have such strong, long relationships, friendships. With Tip Toe, David Morrissey and I have known each other since the late '80s. Acting against each other in such a horrible way was so great because we were so close and trusted each other. And that was a really good thing about being older—you're so sure of someone and so trust someone. Also, I just love having a life where I do have friends in their 20s and 30s and 40s.

I equate oldness with lack of curiosity. You can be very old and still be very curious, but whenever anyone says, “Oh, I'm too old for this,” I think it's like a death knell somewhere. A little fairy dies when you say that.

I enjoy not giving so much of a fuck. I'm really happy in my life and things don't bother me as much as they used to. I think it'll all come out in the wash. We'll work it out. I feel I'm much more sanguine about things, especially as I've got such a crazy life with so many things going on. It's always this sort of mental schedule and trying to make it all work, but I just now think, “It'll be fine.” So that's a good thing about being older.

MH: What are you most curious about these days?

AC: Lots of things. I'm running a theater now and every day there are things I've got to deal with and talk about and find out about, and that's amazing. I joined TikTok today, so I decided to do that. I want to learn to play the bagpipes. I want to learn to type properly because, I mean, I use my computer, obviously, and I've written books and films, but I've never really properly learned to type. And I want to get better at Spanish, and I have a sort of trad wife fantasy of making gluten-free scones in my AGA, which is a kind of country stove.

AC: I don't really feel this age, but when I was 33, I moved to New York, and I feel like my life hasn't really changed that much since then. I still live in the same area. I still have a lot of the same friends. I do a lot of the same things. I have the same kind of life. I don't go out as much as I used to, but I love dancing still, and I would do that more if people didn't take photos of me all the time. Before mobile phone cameras, you didn't have to worry about being off your tits and dancing in a club with your chums. Now you do.

I'm obviously not 33. I don't look 33. But I feel like I'm as fit as I was when I was 33, kind of.

This interview has been edited for length and clarity.

#4996. Fashion Maverick Christopher John Rogers Unveils Bold New Carpets for The Rug Company

For Christopher John Rogers, the magic of a garment lies in its making. “I was thinking about preserving what happens in the studio—draping, cutting, chalk lines, thread—and the mistakes, smears, and imperfections that are left by the hand,” notes the Louisiana-born, New York–based fashion designer. That ethos became the departure point for his new collection, The Studio by Christopher John Rogers for The Rug Company. Conceived in partnership with the brand’s creative director, Will Cooper, the line builds upon The Rug Company’s history of collaborations with fashion greats such as Alexander McQueen, Diane von Furstenberg, and Vivienne Westwood.

Marker Test rug showcased in Will Cooper's apartment

Another rug from The Studio By Christopher John Rogers for The Rug Company collection in Cooper's living area

Spanning a range of flatweaves and plush piles, crafted using hand-knotted and woven techniques, Rogers’s six designs reference the abstract paintings of Morris Louis and Ellsworth Kelly in addition to his own archival runway looks and creative process. “The patterns came from something as simple as my marker tests on paper,” he says, recalling how he reinterpreted those scribbles, which date to his 2022 pre-fall runway collection, in silk and wool. “I’ve always loved the idea that something accidental can become the very thing you want to preserve.”

Whereas Paintstroke evokes a wet stripe of pigment, Chalk Line juxtaposes raised cross-hatchings against a neutral ground. Mixing faded primary colors with warm earth tones, the palette channels Rogers’s signature hues through a subdued lens, the construction of each piece adding a sense of patina. “We wanted the rugs to feel like they could be from any decade; they could be vintage pieces,” says Cooper, who was introduced to Rogers by past brand collaborator Farrow & Ball. “They reflect Christopher’s evolution and traces of the many hands involved in their making—histories that ultimately become part of the home.”

#4995. New retro electric motorcycle to debut at EICMA with a novel feature: a second gear

Most electric motorcycles have something in common: a single-speed transmission. But a British company is preparing to shake things up at this year’s EICMA motorcycle show in Milan, where it will unveil a new electric motorcycle demonstrator featuring an unusual two-speed transmission designed to shift gears without interrupting power delivery.

The company behind the technology, E-Motiv Shift Systems, has developed what it calls a seamless powershift transmission, promising improved acceleration, better efficiency, and a more engaging riding experience.

Unlike conventional motorcycle transmissions, which generally require a brief interruption in power during gear changes, E-Motiv’s system is designed to maintain torque throughout the shift.

That could offer some interesting advantages for electric motorcycles, where a lower first gear could provide stronger acceleration while a taller second gear could help optimize motor efficiency at higher speeds.

Of course, most electric motorcycles don’t actually need multiple gears, and very few have ever offered multi-speed transmissions. Electric motors produce strong torque across a wide operating range, which is why manufacturers generally prefer the simplicity, lower weight, and reduced maintenance of single-speed drivetrains.

But that doesn’t mean a second gear couldn’t offer some benefits.

E-Motiv’s technology actually traces its roots back nearly 25 years to the Zeroshift transmission system, which eventually found its way into Aprilia’s MotoGP racing motorcycles in 2015.

It's possible, from my perspective, that running a motor at lower rpm under reduced load like flat terrain cruising, could improve range, and lower temperatures, and might be beneficial for cars and trucks as well.

The latest electric version was developed through a £1 million UK government-funded project involving motorcycle manufacturer BSA, the University of Bath, and the Institute of Advanced Automotive Propulsion Systems (IAAPS).

The demonstrator motorcycle, known as Demo 1, was conceived by British design consultancy Envisage and is being built by IAAPS. Real-world testing is scheduled to begin in early 2027.

E-Motiv will showcase the technology at EICMA from November 3-8, though the demonstrator remains a development project rather than a production-ready motorcycle.

Whether two-speed transmissions eventually become common on electric motorcycles remains to be seen. But if E-Motiv can deliver meaningful efficiency and performance improvements without adding too much weight, cost, or complexity, it could give future electric motorcycles another interesting trick up their sleeves.

From smart swim goggles to self-cleaning vacuums.

DO YOU REALLY need an app for everything? Mileseey Golf GeneSonic Pro Detachable GPS Speaker

Panasonic ARC5 PRO Men's 5-Blade Electric Razor

Add a Hue Bridge and an HDMI Sync Box or compatible Hue Sync TV app setup, and the colors follow the action on screen. Keep work meetings and school pickups on the same page. This 27-inch touchscreen brings the family’s calendars together in one spot, with colored schedules you can check on your way out. Shared grocery lists and chore charts help everyone see what needs doing. Add a paid Plus subscription for meal planning and automatic event imports from emails, PDFs, and photos. Give it a spot in the kitchen for a quick look at the day over coffee.

Cold garage? Outdoor project that can’t wait until spring? This heated shirt jacket brings warmth to the job, with three heat settings and up to eight hours of runtime on low using the included rechargeable battery. Expect four hours on medium or two on high. Durable pockets hold your essentials, and a concealed battery pocket keeps the power source tucked away. Like any good work shirt, it has a roomy fit that lets you reach and bend.

Logitech Signature Comfort Plus Combo MK880

Long days at your desk? This curved keyboard has a cushioned palm rest and quiet keys so you won’t disturb your officemates. The included mouse has cushioning of its own, plus a scroll wheel for cruising through long documents. Both connect through Bluetooth and can switch between up to three paired devices, handy when you’re bouncing between your work laptop and personal computer.

See more of that spreadsheet without constantly scrolling. This 28.2-inch monitor has a taller screen than most widescreen displays, giving you extra room for documents or two windows side by side. Text looks crisp, and a matte finish helps cut reflections. Brightness adjusts to the room, while a light behind the screen adds a soft glow to your desk. One USB-C connection can carry your laptop’s picture and charge it at the same time.

Finding your wallet just got easier. This 1.7-millimeter-thick tracker slips into a card slot without the lump of a round tracker and works with Apple Find My or Google Find Hub. If it’s hiding nearby, ring the built-in alarm to narrow the search. Wireless charging saves you from replacing batteries, with up to five months between charges. A small addition that could save you a frantic morning.

#4992. Ford casts 5,000 ‘unicastings’ for Fathom EV truck in Tesla-style shift

Ford has now produced more than 5,000 large aluminum “unicastings” for the Fathom, its upcoming $28,350 midsize electric pickup, as it fine-tunes the process ahead of a 2027 launch.

Each truck gets two of them, front and rear, replacing 146 individual parts. It’s the clearest sign yet that Ford is all-in on the manufacturing approach Tesla popularized with its Giga Press six years ago.

The update comes from Vladimir Bogachuk, Ford’s chief engineer of advanced vehicle structure architecture, in a post on Ford’s From the Road blog. The castings are made at Ford’s Shelbyville Technical Center and shipped to Louisville Assembly Plant for process testing and prototype builds. Louisville is the Kentucky factory Ford is retooling for the Fathom and other vehicles on its Universal EV Platform.

Ford melts aluminum ingots at about 1,300°F (700°C) and injects the metal into either a front or rear structure mold. Once a robot pulls the part out, it goes into a quench tank, gets trimmed and machined, and then runs a gauntlet of checks: industrial X-ray scans for pores and voids, dimensional measurements in a climate-controlled room, optical surface scans, and lab tensile and chemistry tests on samples.

Nothing ships to Louisville until a batch clears every inspection. Ford says it’s also working on cutting cycle time and on designing the castings for “modular repairability.”

That last part has been the main reason why automakers have been reticent about larger cast parts. Repair cost is the biggest knock on giant castings, since a damaged one-piece structure can turn a moderate crash into a write-off. Lucid executive Cory Steuben has said insurance concerns drove the company’s decision to skip gigacasting altogether.

Tesla kicked this off in 2020 with the first IDRA Giga Press at Fremont, a machine with roughly 6,000 tons of clamping force. It cast the Model Y’s rear underbody in a single piece, replacing 70 parts, and Elon Musk said the change removed the need for about 300 robots.

Tesla wanted to go much further. It added a front casting, tied the two together with a structural battery pack, and moved to even bigger presses for the Cybertruck. But in 2024, Reuters reported that Tesla had backed away from its plan to cast nearly the entire underbody in one piece, and the automaker has reportedly since dropped the front casting on some Model Y production.

So Ford’s 146-to-2 figure looks great on paper. But it’s a front-and-rear layout, basically the same architecture Tesla launched with on the Model Y. Ford isn’t leapfrogging Tesla here. It’s catching up, just with a vehicle that will cost less than any Tesla on sale.

Since that first Giga Press, a growing list of automakers have bought huge presses or committed to them:

Anyone have real world experience with how much the cost of repairability with Tesla Gigacasting? As mentioned in article likely to get totaled by insurance and/or expensive to fix compared to the traditional modular approach, shifting cost to consumer to reduce corporate costs.

Others aren’t buying it. Stellantis has said it won’t invest in gigacasting, while BMW and Mercedes-Benz have stuck with smaller presses for body parts. Hyundai pushed its “Hypercasting” plan back two years.

We’ve been tracking this casting race since Tesla’s first Giga Press showed up in Fremont, and the lesson so far is simple. Fewer parts is great, but the press alone doesn’t make a car cheap. The whole factory does.

That’s why Ford’s approach is worth watching. The unicastings are one piece of a bigger overhaul at Louisville, where Ford is splitting the line into three branches (front, rear, and the battery pack with the interior floor) that meet at the end. Ford says that can be up to 40% faster than its current assembly, or about 15% net once it reinvests some of the savings into automation and insourcing. It’s very close to the logic behind Tesla’s “unboxed” process, and they are betting it’s what actually gets you to a $28,350 truck.

#4989. In This Midcentury Nashville Home a Mosaic Rainbow-Hued Window Brings Color to the Earthy Interiors

Here’s one you may not have heard before: What do Serge Mouille sconces, a Louis Poulsen artichoke pendant, and Borge Mogensen dining chairs have in common? Hint, hint: They all live in the Nashville home of Doug and Carina Jenkins, the founders of antiques and vintage furniture store Eneby Home.

One might reasonably presume the home of antiques purveyors to be chock-full of one-of-a-kind finds from far-flung locales, and while that is certainly the case here, the husband-and-wife duo’s house otherwise expertly subverts expectations.

Built in 1970, the uber-quiet four-acre property sits at the top of a hill overlooking seemingly endless rolling valleys. It’s surrounded by native honeysuckle bushes and Tennessee Fieldstone, and the sun sets on the front side of the home at 6:37 p.m., says Doug, who dreamed of building what he describes as an “art gallery” for their furniture.

The Jenkinses kept the wall of original Tennessee Fieldstone—and the original copper fireplace—but added their own flair with vintage furnishings, like a La Mura sofa, Gianfranco Frattini for Cassina leather lounge chairs, a Charles Dudouyt credenza, and a 1960s marble fossil coffee table from Italy. “It’s super unique,” says Doug. “It doesn’t have a name behind it.”

Doug and Carina Jenkins, the founders of Eneby Home, with their daughters. They kept the original Tennessee Fieldstone on the wall and paired it with an antique marble dining table that’s “huge and heavy,” says Doug. “The guys hated bringing stuff to our house.” Even though the Louis Poulsen artichoke pendant is often seen in copper, the Jenkins were able to source one with a green finish, seen here. The chairs are Borge Mogensen; the 1960s geometric painting is from France.

A well-lit workspace features a vintage 1950s desk, an Italian cocoon lamp, and a Hans Wegner chair from Denmark.

“There’s not a lot of this type of house in Nashville,” says Greg Dutton, the founder and architect behind Columbus, Ohio-based and AD PRO Directory design firm Dutton Studio. “But we had to look past what was there—a mixed bag with a new era in each room—to create a backdrop for the work they do.”

Cue the blank slate. The couple bought the house as-is—black toilets, mustard-hued siding, and early 2000s kitchen included—and embarked on a “bigger than expected” renovation, says Doug, that translated to moving walls, updating floor plans, a new roof and flooring, and overall furnishings, of course.

With the resulting minimalist white walls and clean lines, how does one amass the amount of character evident here? “When you build in materials that have inherent imperfection, that naturally bakes in a lot of warmth,” says Dutton. Think locally sourced, character-grade white oak flooring throughout; imported reclaimed European bluestone that’s well over 100 years old in the primary bath; and an original copper fireplace in the living room.

“The mosaic supposedly came from a building in Bologna,” says Doug. “The morning sun comes up on this side” and ignites a fire of filtered light within the home.

“The island has a commercial look to it,” says Doug of the Vipp system. “It hit every box. It’s sick.” Dutton helped reconfigure the space’s floor plan to open the room to the dining area. The oven is by Miele.

A Guillerme et Chambron cabinet, sourced from Eneby Home, houses curiosities. The 1960s pendant light is Stilnovo.

The guest bathroom originally had a black toilet and smoked glass. Now it features vintage Serge Mouille sconces, of which Doug has “never seen another pair,” a 250-year-old antique Italian mirror from the 1800s, and a Josef Hoffman bench.

“Flaws make the house feel better, not worse,” says Dutton. “But creating something that looks effortless actually takes a lot of coordination. We wanted it to feel like it had just happened that way, and installing materials that feel like they’ve been there forever automatically gives the house permanence and ensures it’ll age gracefully over time.”

The push-and-pull between old and new, simple and full of character, clean and distressed is one the Jenkins family is familiar with. The balancing act makes the house interesting, says Doug, and the same inclination to lean on historic materials translates just as well to furnishings.

“Some of this stuff we’d been saving in hopes we’d one day be able to use it for our own house,” says Doug. One such example is a 1940s or ’50s-era, rainbow-hued mosaic window the couple bought five years ago and stored in a crate ever since. “We asked Greg to change the layout of the wall to make it work,” says Doug. The stone sculpture on display in front of the window is another; it’s 2,000 years old, from Rome.

An earthy palette in the primary bedroom lets the landscape through the windows do the talking. The sconces are by Biny; the oversized 1880 landscape painting is from France; the chairs are Borge Mogensen; the side table is Charles Dudouyt, and the floor lamp is La Borne.

“The fireplace is original, and we ran with it,” says Doug. “It’s New Mexico style and is super cool.” It’s paired with an abstract geometric art piece and a collection of vases from Denmark.

The stainless steel Vipp island in the kitchen, which the couple found in Denmark, is “the best thing in the house,” says Carina. “We moved from a house with a big pantry, and it was a challenge to figure out where everything would go. This island holds a lot.” Here, the family embraces the “European model” of compact living–albeit in 4,500 square feet. Dutton designed the black cabinetry wall as a backdrop for the island, which he calls a “piece of jewelry.”

Like any gallerist worth his salt, Doug spends five minutes each night turning off all of the ambient lighting in the house, like a pair of midcentury Biny sconces in the primary bedroom, an Orocolo lamp in the living room, and a 1970s Marika Carniti Bollea Nautilus lamp in the kitchen. Much of the light shines on a collection of artwork that’s so vast it’s often swapped out.

“We’ve already traded a few things,” says Doug. “If we find something we love, we want to use it. It’s what we do. It’s a perk, for sure.”

As is a home that’s forever. “Our girls want to come here when we’re old with their own kids,” says Carina. “They love it so much.”

The 1950s painting is from Denmark, the 1970s vase is from Sweden, the 1970s stool is Pierre Chapo, and the ceramic is from France. “We spend a lot of time in Denmark and like to mix Danish and Swedish,” says Doug. “We know a lot about that style of furniture.”

Dutton reworked the floor plan in the primary bathroom to set the vanity under a skylight. “We don’t use much overhead lighting,” says Carina.

#4987. Tesla confirms Vietnam entry to take on VinFast and its 36% market share

Tesla has started hiring in Vietnam, and one of its recruiters says the company is now “officially coming” to the country. The five open roles include a sales manager, a service manager, and a charging project developer.

It’s a tough market to walk into. VinFast, the local all-electric automaker, has sold roughly 36% of every new car in Vietnam this year, more than its next five competitors combined.

Tesla quietly registered Tesla Motors Vietnam LLC in Ho Chi Minh City on September 11, with about 77.7 billion dong (roughly $3 million) in charter capital and a business scope covering car imports, wholesale, and retail. Tesla never commented on it.

Now we have the first confirmation from someone inside the company. Ricky Ip, who recruits for Tesla, posted on X this morning:

Tesla is officially coming to Vietnam – and we're hiring.Our mission is to build a world of amazing abundance and we are looking for passionate people to join us in Vietnam and help make that vision a reality. Current Job Openings:Sales Manager – https://t.co/AvW3higssS… pic.twitter.com/YDZvLNmz8P

The five openings are Sales Manager, Service Manager, HR Business Partner, Purchasing Manager, and Project Developer, Charging. That last one is the tell. You don’t hire someone to develop charging projects unless you plan to build Superchargers in the country.

Tesla has followed this playbook before in Southeast Asia. It went on a hiring spree in Thailand in September 2022 and opened orders there three months later. It started hiring in the Philippines in September 2024 and launched in early 2025. So a Vietnam launch in the next few months wouldn’t be a surprise.

There’s no word yet on pricing, models, or store locations. Oddly, the recruiting graphic features the next-generation Roadster, with fine print saying the vehicle is “for illustration purposes only.” Fair enough.

Tesla isn’t walking into an empty market. It’s walking into VinFast’s backyard.

VinFast delivered 177,697 cars in Vietnam through the first nine months of 2026, out of a national total of about 495,000. That’s about 36% of the entire car market, gas cars included. In September alone, it had 37.7% of the market.

Here’s how the year-to-date numbers stack up against the next brands:

Add up Toyota, Hyundai, Mitsubishi, Ford, and Kia and you get 175,136. VinFast alone beat all five of them.

And it’s already passed its full-year 2025 volume with a quarter left to go. VinFast says September was its 25th straight month as Vietnam’s best-selling car brand.

The model mix tells you a lot about the market. The VinFast VF 3, a tiny two-door electric crossover that launched at under $13,000, is Vietnam’s best-selling vehicle so far this year. VinFast models held the top five spots in September.

VinFast builds its cars in Haiphong. Tesla would have to import everything, most likely from Giga Shanghai, and Vietnam kept passenger cars on its high-tariff list under the ASEAN-China free trade agreement.

Expanding to Vietnam is a logical step considering the other markets nearby that Tesla is already selling in. In these markets, a smaller and affordable car (model 2) would have been a big seller.

Charging is another gap. VinFast’s sister company V-Green runs the country’s dominant charging network and announced plans this year to spend about $380 million on 99 ultra-fast charging stations. Third-party networks in Vietnam are still thin.

There is one thing working in Tesla’s favor. Vietnam just extended its registration fee exemption for battery-electric cars through the end of 2030, and that applies to imported EVs too.

And VinFast’s volume comes with an asterisk. A significant share of its deliveries go to GSM, the Xanh SM electric taxi and ride-hailing company founded by VinFast chairman Pham Nhat Vuong. VinFast has long relied on Vietnam for the bulk of its deliveries, which we pointed out when it delivered nearly 100,000 EVs in 2024.

#4985. CATL pushes ahead with 20 Ah solid-state EV batteries, with 500 Wh/kg in sight

CATL hit another milestone in its solid-state battery development, advancing to 20 Ah cells. The world’s leading battery maker aims to begin small-scale production in 2027.

CATL remains the leading global EV battery manufacturer by a wide margin. According to SNE Research, CATL held a commanding 39.9% share of the global EV battery market through the first half of 2026.

To stay ahead, the company is advancing several new battery chemistries, including solid-state and sodium-ion, promising to be safer, more efficient, and lower cost than traditional lithium-ion batteries.

When investors asked about the progress of its solid-state battery program on Tuesday, CATL said it continues to invest heavily in all-solid-state batteries and expects to begin small-scale production in 2027.

As it has stated before, output is expected to be limited at first as it scales up to mass production around 2030.

Speaking at the Summer Davos Forum in June, CATL chairman and CEO Robin Zeng said that, on a scale of one to nine, all-solid-state batteries have only reached level four.

All-solid-state batteries differ from semi-solid-state batteries in that they require a solid electrolyte to conduct lithium ions, rather than a liquid.

The company uses a sulfide-based solid electrolyte system, which offers high ionic conductivity and deformability, but maintaining stable contact between solid components remains a hurdle.

CATL said its sulfide-based batteries have now entered 20 Ah trial production as it aims to achieve an energy density of 500 Wh/kg.

CATL’s 2027 timeline to begin small-scale production aligns with other automakers and battery manufacturers racing to introduce all-solid-state EV batteries.

Toyota, which has been promising solid-state batteries for nearly a decade now, aims to launch the first all-solid-state battery-powered EV in 2027 or 2028.

Using Sumitomo Metal Mining’s proprietary powder synthesis technology, Toyota claimed to have developed a “highly durable cathode material” for all-solid-state batteries last October. Toyota revealed a prototype last year, claiming it can deliver 745 miles (1,200 km) of range and charge in under 10 minutes.

However, like CATL, Toyota expects production to be limited and will not reach the mass market until closer to the end of the decade.

Samsung SDI, on the other hand, aims to begin mass production of its SolidStack all-solid-state batteries in the second half of 2027.

BYD, which ranks second in global EV battery sales behind CATL, also plans to launch its first solid-state battery-powered vehicle in 2027.

In an exclusive interview with Carwow.es last month, executive vice president Stella Li said BYD is in “the leading position” on solid-state batteries and will “prove that” next year.

BYD’s battery division, FinDreams, aims to begin producing sulfide-based all-solid-state batteries in limited batches in 2027, ramping up to mass production by 2030.

While solid-state batteries are often hailed the “holy grail” of battery tech, they are only one part of the equation.

It took them about 3 years for pilot to production of LMFP so I believe it. I generally believe CATL and BYD along with many of the others anyway. You can trust the big Chinese suppliers and OEMs on time line and what they have for the most part. Everyone else not a chance. I think Ford figured that out with suppliers.

CATL, BYD, and others continue advancing lithium iron phosphate (LFP) and sodium-ion batteries, which will ultimately create more efficient, lower-cost options that offer various performance needs.

BYD’s second-generation Blade LFP batteries already deliver over 1,000 km (621 miles) of CLTC range. What’s next?

One thing is certain: New battery chemistries and evolving tech will only continue making EVs more efficient, unlocking longer driving ranges, faster charging speeds, and better performance.

#4982. Victorian Architecture Meets Mediterranean Charm in This Warm and Whimsical London Home

Interior designer Marta de la Rica describes herself as “a New Yorker by birth, French by adoption, and Spanish at heart.” That mix of cultures would prove to be an asset for one of her recent projects: the 5,382-square-foot four-story home of a family of four in London. A fine example of Victorian architecture, the building, located in the quiet residential neighborhood of Battersea and only steps away from Battersea Park, was built in 1879. “I already knew the clients, since I had previously designed their home in Bilbao, and I knew what kind of atmosphere the family wanted as well as what was particularly important to them about their home,” says de la Rica.

The walls of the stairwell are painted in Yel-01 Smooth by Clayworks and the Clam Shell Large light fixtures are by Porta Romana. The floor is made of several different marbles: Negro Marquina, Perlado, and Rojo Alicante.

The young family is originally from Spain but had already been living in London for nearly 10 years when they decided to buy a single-family home there. De la Rica decided to design spaces for them with a British-Mediterranean look that reflects the character of the historic Victorian architecture and the demands of modern family life. “The family wanted an elegant and peaceful home that, above all, would be warm and cozy,” says de la Rica. “For us, that meant creating spaces with comfort, character, and personality.”

Two midcentury armchairs sit in front of a Louis XIV fireplace from 1700.

Light brown and rust tones dominate the living room’s color palette.

Since the clients are Spanish, the project had a particularly interesting cultural dimension for the designer, as she explains: “We wanted the house to feel personal to them while also remaining true to its London identity and the Victorian architecture. So the project became a dialogue between English interior design and a more relaxed, Mediterranean lifestyle,” says de la Rica.

De la Rica’s interiors combine English interior design and a relaxed lifestyle in this Victorian building where the sunroom doubles as a dining room.

Originally, the family had been looking for a home in a different part of London, but when they saw this house, they were immediately taken with it and changed their plans. The building’s structure was in very good condition; only the floor plan needed to be reconfigured. “Above all, we wanted to preserve the spacious rooms, the ceiling heights, the bay windows, and the staircase,” explains the designer.

A relaxed breakfast setting with chairs by Vintola Studio and Benjamin pendant lights from Felix Lighting,

Visually, all the rooms have an almost opulent feel, yet at the same time, each one reflects its particular purpose. The ground floor includes a study, a living room, a kitchen, af amily room, and a sunroom, which also serves as the dining room. The parents’ bedroom is located on the second floor. A separate children’s area with bedrooms and play areas was created on the top floor. “The sunroom is a big part of the character of the house. To build the basement, it had to be demolished and rebuilt. It was very important to us to preserve the extraordinary presence of this space and its connection to the rest of the house,” says de la Rica.

The designer made the transitions from one room to the next as natural as possible by ensuring that the materials and atmosphere flow seamlessly.

The living spaces flow into the garden (designed by Matthew Wilson) and the terraces in front of the family room and the sunroom provide direct access to the outdoors. The garden features a pool, an outdoor kitchen, and a spacious dining area, providing additional spaces for social gatherings. The interior is characterized by elegant craftsmanship, custom details, warm colors, and high-quality materials. These choices highlight the grandeur of the historic home while also creating a cozy atmosphere.

A 20th-century dresser serves as a nightstand in the primary bedroom; an onyx lamp sits next to a vase filled with flowers.

“I particularly like that the rooms all have their own character, yet there is a clear, unifying design that carries throughout the house,” says the designer. It was also important to de la Rica to emphasize the scale of the house without having it feel cold or ostentatious. “Even in the larger rooms, there’s a coziness. It was very important to us to achieve this balance,” she says.

In the guest bathroom, the walls are painted in Yel-01 Smooth by Clayworks. Below the Negro Marquina marble sink by MDLR-Design, a curtain of Marly fabric by Colony.

The playroom showcases the Victorian architecture.

The children’s rooms are located under the mansard roof, which is typical of many Victorian houses in London. For the two youngest members of the family, the designer transformed the entire top floor with its sloped ceilings into colorful playrooms.

Amid dinosaurs and Mickey Mouse toys, the family’s son sleeps here. A Globe ceiling light hangs above the bed (Olofane).

De la Rica’s favorite parts of the house, however, are the living room and the dining room. For her, these are the areas where the home truly comes to life and where the design directly interacts with the family’s daily routine.

Above the large sofa in the family room, a Soren globe light from Pinch. The cream-colored Mayan Cuzco rug is from Bellbridge.

She particularly likes that the living room, dining room, and kitchen form a single, open space. “This part of the house allows for a very uncomplicated way of living,” she explains. “It’s very beautiful but not contrived or overly formal. It exudes coziness and spontaneity, and for me, that’s ultimately what makes a family home.”

For the walls in this bathroom, the designer chose Oli-03 Smooth by Clayworks, wall sconces by Vaughan, and a Calacatta Viola marble sink.

The wardrobe in the guest bedroom is upholstered with Ticking Stripe 1 Black fabric (Ian Mankin). The decorative Flax 03 tassels are from Samuel & Sons.

In the daughter’s bedroom, the rug is from Kvadrat. The closet doors are upholstered with fabrics from Yutes.

The Bert & May tiles in Garnet and Sand White were custom-made for this bathroom. The bathtub is Perlado marble.

#4979. Tesla’s rocket-shaped Robotaxi map in Houston is partly ‘fake’

Tesla’s new rocket-shaped Robotaxi service area in Houston includes parts of downtown where the service won’t actually pick you up, according to Ethan McKanna, the creator of Robotaxi Tracker and former Tesla employee.

McKanna rode the service after Monday’s expansion and called Houston “by far the most egregious ‘fake’ AV service area” he has seen. One trip quote had him spending about 40 minutes in a Robotaxi to go two blocks.

On Monday, Tesla expanded its Houston Robotaxi geofence into the shape of a rocket. It took the small launch zone in northwest Harris County and stretched it into a long corridor down to downtown and Bellaire, announced with “Houston, we have liftoff.”

McKanna runs Robotaxi Tracker, the crowdsourced site that tracks Tesla’s Robotaxi fleet and service areas, and he worked at Tesla this summer, so he knows the program better than most. He went to Houston to try it out. Here’s what he posted last night:

Another thing. Often AV service area maps advertised are kind of fake- they don’t actually go to everywhere in the polygon the provider publishes. Robotaxi Houston is probably by far the most egregious ‘fake’ AV service area I’ve seenI get they were doing it so they could get… https://t.co/ILXIkmwGVp pic.twitter.com/yUfswZhQKF

The video shows the Robotaxi app with a pickup pin dropped in downtown Houston, well inside the polygon Tesla published. The app refuses it: “Location not available. Move the map to select a different location.”

McKanna acknowledged that AV service area maps are often “kind of fake,” since operators don’t serve every street inside the polygon they advertise. But he singled out Houston as the worst case he’s seen and said he understands Tesla did it “so they could get the starship shape,” which doesn’t seem like a good reason to fake availability.

The day before, he posted a trip quote that shows what this looks like for a rider:

40 min ride to cross 2 blocks downtown.this is pretty rough pic.twitter.com/n8kP4s9evT

The app quoted a 2:05 pm pickup on the east side of downtown and a 2:48 pm drop-off at Houston Police Department headquarters on Travis Street, a couple of blocks away. Instead of cutting across downtown, the planned route runs south through Midtown and the Museum District to the Texas Medical Center area, then loops back north.

McKanna’s read is that the Robotaxi appears to be avoiding railroad crossings downtown, and he said it fits with “other strange routing in Houston.” He expects Tesla to fix it.

To be fair, he wasn’t down on the service overall. He wrote that, apart from that issue, it “feels really good in Houston,” with reasonable wait times and “significantly better driving than my recent Waymo rides.”

Tesla isn’t the only one. On Tuesday, Robotaxi Tracker reported that Waymo’s Orlando service area had shrunk by 10 square miles, down 8.4% to 109 square miles. McKanna explained what happened:

Orlando’s airport was inside the map Waymo published, but McKanna checked the app and it won’t let riders set the airport as a drop-off yet. So the tracker took it out. Waymo’s airport service might be rolling out soon, and someone got ahead of the announcement.

That’s an airport on the edge of the map. In Houston, the dead zone is in the middle of downtown.

The key to understanding Tesla is the stockholders are the real customers, Elon looks at actual paying customers of his products as patsies or marks who will willingly serve as unpaid beta (or alpha) testers and who will be the ones paying or going to jail when something goes wrong.

First, a big thanks to Ethan. Tesla shares very little data about its Robotaxi program, and Robotaxi Tracker has become the best independent source on where Tesla’s fleet actually operates and how big it is. And as Orlando shows, he holds Waymo to the same standard.

Every AV operator has gaps in its map, Waymo included. But Waymo’s Orlando gap is an airport on the edge of the map that it’s likely about to add. Tesla’s Houston gap is downtown, and it drew a rocket first and figured out where the cars could actually go second.

Service and product should always outrank marketing, and that’s clearly not the case with Tesla’s Robotaxi. People rely on those maps to know if Tesla offers the service where they are. Faking the map just to make a rocket shape is completely stupid. Do better.

If you’re thinking about an EV to get around town instead of waiting on a Robotaxi, powering it with home solar can cut your fuel costs to nearly zero. With electricity rates up almost 10% last year and expected to keep climbing, going solar is one of the best ways to protect yourself against rising costs. And with lease and PPA options, you can do it with zero upfront cost and start saving immediately. If you want to find the best deal, check out EnergySage. It’s a free service with hundreds of pre-vetted installers competing for your business, so you save 20 to 30% compared to going it alone. No sales calls until you pick an installer. #4975. Inside the Underground Mosques of Kazakhstan’s Surreal Mangystau Desert

Up until about 20 years ago, a faint trail up a steep hill through limestone-and-chalk cliffs was the only way to reach Beket-Ata, one of the sacred underground mosques—as they’re colloquially known—of Kazakhstan’s Mangystau desert. “There was no proper path then, but people made the difficult climb in the heat and wind,” my guide, Yerzhan Nagmanov, tells me as we embark on our way to the holy site—eased slightly by the stairs now carved into the stone. That arduous journey, the travelers believed, “was part of the pilgrimage.”

En route to Beket-Ata with Mangystau Tour 999, visitors walk a trail cutting through the steep hill.

The trail of stairs descending into the canyon en route to Beket-Ata.

As inhospitable as the roughly 64,000-square-mile desert may seem, it has attracted scores of people over the years arriving to see Beket-Ata and the four other sacred complexes scattered across its expanse: Shopan-Ata, Karaman-Ata, Shakpak-Ata and Sultan-Epe. Traveling between the five holy sites, the distinction between architecture and geology evaporates into the desert heat: Walls, ceilings, and floors are all variations of the rock from which the mosques were excavated. For pilgrims, the journey to these sacred sites is an act of devotion, where people come to honor revered Sufi masters, pray for blessings, and seek spiritual guidance in times of difficulty.

The mosques are hundreds of years old, collectively dating between the 12th and 18th centuries, but they’re new to the UNESCO World Heritage List as of this summer. The “Rock Mosques and Associated Sacred Sites of Mangystau” were added to the esteemed collection of landmarks in July.

Each mosque is associated with a different spiritual master within Sufism, the mystical branch of Islam. The five Sufi masters’ influence shaped the region’s religious landscape, and then the landscape itself, as all of the mosques—crafted in a rock-cut style that grew out of nomadic building traditions—seem to grow directly from the desert terrain; they’re carved into caves, tucked along the contours of cliffs and hillsides, and tunneled below the ground.

After three weeks travelling through Kazakhstan’s cities, mountains, and pine forests, I spent my final week in Mangystau, a landscape so different from anything I’d seen so far in the country that it felt like a whole world unto itself. Having recently visited Astana’s Grand Mosque, with its blue domes, soaring minarets, and gleaming expanses of marble, I wondered whether Mangystau’s rock-cut shrines might fall flat by comparison. For me, however, their lack of splashy grandeur is precisely what made them so compelling.

A stately arch welcomes travelers to the steps leading to Beket-Ata.

While I am not Muslim, I was fascinated by how the Sufi masters traversed this unforgiving terrain along with their families. Some walked hundreds of kilometers to establish places of worship far from the comforts of the world, spending years in prayer and contemplation before eventually being buried here. I was drawn to the way the rock mosques embody a humble yet stunning expression of faith shaped by the austere landscape, where instead of gilded shrines or massive palaces, devotion found a more organic form. Through guided 4x4 tours with Red Maya Travel and Mangystau Tour 999, I visited all five.

A necropolis inside the Shopan-Ata complex.

My first stop on the tour is Shopan-Ata, named after the 12th-century Sufi teacher. The complex consists of a vast necropolis (a large burial ground with tombs of religious figures and generations of pilgrims) of more than 2,000 monuments, including domed mausoleums and kulpytas, which are ornately carved stone steles traditionally used as Kazakh gravestones. The 12-chamber mosque is carved into the rock, and a shaft of daylight is cast onto two sacred wooden poles below. According to a local tradition, pilgrims recite a prayer and circle the poles three times counterclockwise, which is believed to help the spirits hear them. Visitors also tie strips of white cloth around the poles, the knot symbolically binding a prayer to the sacred site. These practices likely come from Shamanic and Tengrian traditions of tying strips of cloth to sacred trees and mountain passes to honor nature spirits––customs that became intertwined with local forms of Muslim saint veneration over time.

Nearby, a well offers pilgrims a much-needed respite; these sacred complexes grew around the few natural resources that made life in the desert possible.

Domed mausoleums at the Shopan-Ata necropolis.

The entrance to Shopan-Ata, shaded by a mulberry tree. These mosques were built around oases that sustained life in the harsh desert climate.

Legend holds that centuries later, Shopan-Ata appeared to Beket-Ata in a dream, offering spiritual guidance, making this shrine an important stop before continuing to Beket-Ata. Nearby, a well offers pilgrims a much-needed respite, its water an incongruous presence in a landscape this dry––a reminder that these sacred complexes grew around the few natural resources that made life in the desert possible.

The sacred wooden pole, around which visitors tie white cloth to seek blessings.

The final few stairs leading up to the Beket-Ata shrine.

Just over an hour away, Beket-Ata is perhaps the best-known of the five mosques. Founded by the 18th-century Sufi teacher Beket Myrzagululy, the shrine lies in a remote canyon about 170 miles from Aktau, the Caspian-side gateway to Mangystau. The steep descent into the canyon is a reminder that the effort needed to reach these shrines has always been a part of the devotion.

The structure is carved into the pale rock, its interconnected chambers following the curves of the cliff. Narrow openings in the ceilings pull shafts of daylight into the cool, dim rooms, where rugs are spread across the stone floor.

Communal dining is integral to Kazakh culture.

An imam is usually on hand to receive visitors and share information about the site. As we settle on the carpets, he details each of the chambers, one of which contains Beket-Ata’s grave, and another is associated with his sister. The remaining rooms are used for prayer, with separate spaces for men and women.

My pilgrimage continues into the dining hall, where a long, continually replenished table runs through the room, with pilgrims sitting on either side and eating together. The mosque provides some freshly cooked meat free of charge, while visitors leave bread, sweets, tea, yogurt, jam and other provisions for those who arrive after them—an especially vivid expression of Central Asian hospitality.

Next we drive across remote country to the Tyub-Karagan Peninsula for Shakpak-Ata, whose cruciform mosque is carved into a massive chalk cliff. Its porous white surface, which looks almost like honeycomb against the desert, was shaped millions of years ago when this part of Mangystau lay beneath the ancient Tethys Sea. Inside, drawings and inscriptions in Arabic, Persian, and Turkic—some dating back to the 18th century—offer a tangible record of the pilgrims who traveled here from across Central Asia, long before air-conditioned 4x4s and Starlink internet made traversing this unforgiving landscape simpler.

A honeycomb-like pattern along the rock face of Shakpak-Ata.

Slightly west, Sultan-Epe lies beside a deep canyon, where a stream threads through thickets of mulberry, buckthorn, blackberry, and wild herbs—another improbable pocket of green in one of Kazakhstan’s driest regions. Sultan-Epe is revered as a protector of seafarers and fishermen—a fitting association for a shrine overlooking the Caspian. According to local tradition, Sultan-Epe was the son of Hakim-Ata, a renowned Sufi poet and disciple of Khoja Ahmed Yasawi, which connects the shrine to the spiritual tradition associated with Shopan-Ata and, through his later spiritual mentor, Beket-Ata.

The stacked-stone entrance to Sultan-Epe.

The final stop on the circuit is Karaman-Ata, where a seven-hectare necropolis stretches across a low, open plot of desert dotted with funerary monuments, some more than 1,000 years old. The mosque is carved into the slope of a small marl hill, and as you descend into the rock, the temperature drops noticeably from the heat outside. A narrow passage leads to a circular room believed to be associated with Sufi rituals including devotional whirling, a type of worship that is expressed through physical movement.

Karaman-Ata, the largest of the five rock mosques of Kazhakstan’s Mangystau desert.

Reaching each site still means long drives across remote terrain, with limited to no mobile coverage or fuel services–making a sturdy vehicle and a trusted local guide essential. But after days spent traveling the region, these places begin to alter your sense of the desert. Around a well, beneath a mulberry tree, or at a table crowded with food and strangers, the landscape that seemed so stark and inhospitable reveals an unexpected generosity, preserved in the rituals of those who continue to make the journey here.

#4972. Piece Together the Perfect Pandora Holiday Gift for Her

From a little sparkle to a personal engraving, these pieces make a point of showing you care.

Great gifts prove you’ve been paying attention. They show you know what brings her joy, what she reaches for every day, and what she’d never think to buy herself. For the women who matter most in your life, that kind of thoughtfulness goes a long way. Mom, for example, deserves something that thanks her for a lifetime of being your first call when something goes wrong. Your sister craves a piece that feels unmistakably her. Your significant other needs something that says more than a dozen roses ever could. The trick is finding a gift with a little staying power, something sentimental, meaningful, and made to last. Jewelry does all three.

This is where Pandora comes in. Founded in Copenhagen in 1982, the jewelry brand has grown from a small Danish shop into a global name, known for its hand-finished designs and signature charm bracelets.

To make the search easier, we’ve picked five pieces for five important women: your mom, sister, significant other, best friend, and daughter. For each one, these sparkling options will become part of her daily rotation, a marker of a particular milestone, and a reminder that the person who gave it to her got it right. Consider this your holiday cheat sheet.

Your sister has always had a way of stealing the spotlight. Might as well give her the earrings to match. The set pairs sterling silver hoops with baguette-cut stones and detachable star charms, so she can switch up her look depending on the occasion. This means she gets two ways to wear them, because even after a lifetime of knowing her, you’re always discovering another side to her that’s worth celebrating.

Some things are better together, including the two of you. This pendant necklace brings three connected stars to life in sterling silver and 14k gold plating, giving the mixed-metal design a little extra dimension. It’s a thoughtful way to celebrate the person who makes your world brighter, shares your best adventures, and puts up with your quirks. The last part alone deserves a gift.

She may be growing up, but she’ll always be your little princess. This ring gives that title a stylish upgrade, with a golden chevron silhouette and a tapered baguette-cut stone at its center. Crafted in 14k gold plating, it’s a sweet reminder that she shouldn’t have to wait for a royal wedding to wear a tiara.

A good best-friend gift proves you know her taste almost as well as she does. The Sparkling Baguette Tennis Bracelet makes a strong case for that, with a sleek row of baguette-cut stones set in 14k gold plating. It’s polished enough to dress up a special occasion outfit, easy enough for every day, and just the sort of thing she’d pick out herself. Knowing her that well? That’s the real flex.

This tag pendant in sterling silver can be engraved with a date, a few meaningful words, or initials that form a message that needs no explanation to anyone else. She can wear it on its own or layer it with her favorite necklaces. The best part? You get to decide what it says. Mom has spent years remembering the little things about you. Now it’s your turn to give her something personal.
